## [2026-05-16] ingest | The Embedded Rust Book — A little C with your Rust
- Source: `raw/book/src/interoperability/c-with-rust.md` → `wiki/sources/rust-embedded-book-interoperability-c-with-rust.md` (file 39/44 of *[[TheEmbeddedRustBook]]* — **first leaf** of the *Interoperability* chapter ([[rust-embedded-book-interoperability-index]]); operationalizes calling **C/C++ from Rust** as two orthogonal halves — the Rust-side interface and the host-side C build).
- Content: **(1) Define the interface.** For every C `typedef struct CoolStruct { int x; int y; }` + `void cool_function(int i, char c, CoolStruct* cs);`, emit `#[repr(C)] pub struct CoolStruct { pub x: cty::c_int, pub y: cty::c_int }` ([[ReprC]]) plus an `extern "C" { pub fn cool_function(i: cty::c_int, c: cty::c_char, cs: *mut CoolStruct); }` block ([[ExternC]]). [[RawPointer|Raw pointers]] (`*mut T` / `*const T`) stand in for Rust references (which C cannot represent); `cty::c_int` / `cty::c_char` aliases stand in for platform-variable C primitives (the [[NoStd|`no_std`]]-compatible equivalent of the `core::ffi` aliases [[rust-embedded-book-interoperability-index]] introduced). Auto-generate the whole interface from C headers via [[Bindgen|`bindgen`]] with `--ctypes-prefix=cty` + `--use-core` flags for `#![no_std]` compatibility. **(2) Build the C/C++ to a static archive.** Three branches: already-prebuilt `lib*.a` (just link); complex `make`/`CMake` project (shell out via `std::process::Command` from [[BuildRs|`build.rs`]] and copy the archive into `target/`); small C source set (compile directly via the [[CcCrate|`cc` crate]] — `fn main() { cc::Build::new().file("src/foo.c").compile("foo"); }`). Two design rules: **C++ has no stable ABI for [[Rustc|rustc]]** → use the `C` ABI as the lingua franca even when wrapping C++ (typically via a thin C wrapper layer); **the package may target `thumbv7m-none-eabi` / [[NoStd|`no_std`]] but `build.rs` itself runs on the *host*** — *"This means you may use any Rust crates which will run on your compilation host."* Static archive (`cool-library.a`) is the embedded delivery format because [[BareMetalProgramming|bare-metal]] firmware has no dynamic loader.
- New pages: **4** — 2 concepts ([[BuildRs]] (Cargo's host-side pre-build hook — runs after deps, before package; three canonical uses: code-gen via [[Bindgen]], shell-out via `std::process::Command`, direct C compile via [[CcCrate|`cc` crate]]; **host/target split** — `build.rs` is `std`-rich on the host even when the package targets `no_std`), [[ExternC]] (the `extern "C"` calling-convention selector — function-ABI companion to [[ReprC]]'s struct-layout discipline; two forms: imported `extern "C" { fn ... }` block and exported `#[no_mangle] pub extern "C" fn`; references cannot cross — get rewritten to [[RawPointer|raw pointers]])) and 2 entities ([[Bindgen]] (`rust-lang/rust-bindgen` — auto-generates `#[repr(C)]` + `extern "C"` from C headers, with `--ctypes-prefix=cty` + `--use-core` for `no_std`), [[CcCrate]] (`alexcrichton/cc-rs` — idiomatic-Rust host C compiler wrapper for `build.rs`; dispatches on `$CC_<target>` for cross-compilation)).
- Updated pages: [[TheEmbeddedRustBook]] — added `rust-embedded-book-interoperability-c-with-rust` to `sources` frontmatter. [[ReprC]] — updated **in-place**: added `rust-embedded-book-interoperability-c-with-rust` to `sources` frontmatter and added [[ExternC]] (calling-convention companion) + [[Bindgen]] (auto-emits `#[repr(C)]`) to connections. [[RawPointer]] — updated **in-place**: added `rust-embedded-book-interoperability-c-with-rust` to `sources` frontmatter and added [[ExternC]] (raw pointers stand in for references in `extern "C"` signatures) + [[Bindgen]] (auto-emits `*mut T` / `*const T` for C `T*`) to connections.

## [2026-05-16] ingest | The Embedded Rust Book — A little Rust with your C
- Source: `raw/book/src/interoperability/rust-with-c.md` → `wiki/sources/rust-embedded-book-interoperability-rust-with-c.md` (file 40/44 of *[[TheEmbeddedRustBook]]* — **second leaf** of the *Interoperability* chapter ([[rust-embedded-book-interoperability-index]]) and the **deliberate mirror** of file 39 ([[rust-embedded-book-interoperability-c-with-rust]]); operationalizes exposing **Rust to C/C++** — the inverse direction of the prior chapter).
- Content: **(1) Build a C-friendly Rust API.** Cargo's `[lib] crate-type = ["cdylib"]` (dynamic — emits `lib<name>.so` / `<name>.dll` / `lib<name>.dylib`) or `["staticlib"]` (static archive — emits `lib<name>.a` / `<name>.lib`) declaration in `Cargo.toml` ([[CrateType]]) selects the output systems-library format; only the first two are C-consumable, and `staticlib` is the only viable form for [[BareMetalProgramming|bare-metal]] firmware. Every exported function carries the canonical attribute triple — `pub` (visibility) + [[NoMangle|`#[no_mangle]`]] (linker — defeats Rust's symbol mangling so the C linker sees the literal source identifier `rust_function` instead of the namespace-and-hash-encoded long form) + [[ExternC|`extern "C"`]] (compiler — switches the function from Rust's unstable internal ABI to the platform C ABI). The canonical shape: `#[no_mangle] pub extern "C" fn rust_function() {}`. This is the **exported** form of `extern "C"` (form 2 of [[ExternC]]) — file 39 covered only the **imported** declaration-block form (form 1). **(2) Embed the produced library in an external build system.** *"This very much depends on your project and/or build system."* Cargo emits the `.so`/`.dll`/`.a` artifact; the host project's `make` / `CMake` / IDE links it like any other system library. The C side needs a matching header — hand-written (`void rust_function();`) or auto-generated by [[Cbindgen|`cbindgen`]] (`github.com/eqrion/cbindgen`), the **inverse-direction** symmetric counterpart to file 39's [[Bindgen|`bindgen`]] (`bindgen`: C → Rust; `cbindgen`: Rust → C). Same C-ABI-as-lingua-franca rule as file 39: *"Because C++ has no stable ABI for the Rust compiler to target, we use `C` for any interoperability between different languages."* Data-marshaling discipline (`#[repr(C)]` on shared structs, `*mut T` / `*const T` raw pointers in place of references, `cty::c_int` aliases for platform-variable C primitives) carries over wholesale from [[rust-embedded-book-interoperability-c-with-rust]].
- New pages: **3** — 2 concepts ([[NoMangle]] (the `#[no_mangle]` symbol-name-preservation attribute — disables Rust's symbol mangling so the C linker sees the literal source identifier; required on every Rust function exported to C, paired with `pub extern "C" fn`; related: `#[link_name = "..."]` for imports and `#[export_name = "..."]` for exports), [[CrateType]] (Cargo's `[lib] crate-type` declaration — selects `cdylib` for dynamic-library output, `staticlib` for static-archive output, `rlib` for the default Rust-internal form; only `cdylib` / `staticlib` are C-consumable, and `staticlib` is the only viable form for [[BareMetalProgramming|bare-metal]] firmware which lacks a dynamic loader)) and 1 entity ([[Cbindgen]] (`eqrion/cbindgen` — the **inverse-direction** codegen tool that scans Rust source for `#[no_mangle] pub extern "C" fn` + `#[repr(C)]` items and emits matching C `.h` / `.hpp` declarations; symmetric counterpart to [[Bindgen]] — `bindgen` is C → Rust, `cbindgen` is Rust → C; a project doing both directions uses both; typically invoked from a [[BuildRs|`build.rs`]] so the generated header tracks the Rust source)).
- Updated pages: [[TheEmbeddedRustBook]] — added `rust-embedded-book-interoperability-rust-with-c` to `sources` frontmatter. [[ExternC]] — updated **in-place**: added `rust-embedded-book-interoperability-rust-with-c` to `sources` frontmatter and added a new *"The two directions — imported vs exported"* section distinguishing form 1 (imported, file 39) from form 2 (exported, file 40); added [[NoMangle]] / [[Cbindgen]] / [[CrateType]] / [[rust-embedded-book-interoperability-rust-with-c]] to connections. [[Bindgen]] — updated **in-place**: added a new *"Symmetric counterpart — `cbindgen`"* section explaining the direction-pair relationship; added [[Cbindgen]] / [[rust-embedded-book-interoperability-rust-with-c]] to connections.

## [2026-05-16] ingest | The Embedded Rust Book — Optimizations
- Source: `raw/book/src/unsorted/speed-vs-size.md` → `wiki/sources/rust-embedded-book-unsorted-speed-vs-size.md` (file 42/44 of *[[TheEmbeddedRustBook]]* — **first substantive leaf** of the *Unsorted topics* chapter, immediately after the trivial placeholder opener at file 41 ([[rust-embedded-book-unsorted-index]])).
- Content: Tactical [[Cargo]] / [[Rustc|`rustc`]] reference on the **five [[OptLevel|`opt-level`]] values** (`0` / `1` / `2` / `3` / `"s"` / `"z"`, inherited from clang/LLVM) across **three regimes** — *No optimizations* (`opt-level = 0`, the `dev` profile default — statement-by-statement debugging, `print` works in [[GDB]], but the binary may not fit in [[FlashMemory|Flash]]); *Optimize for speed* (`1` / `2` / `3`, the `release` profile default at `3` — `2` and `3` **unconditionally unroll loops** with no opt-out, `3` adds vectorization + more inlining; loop unrolling costs *"from 26 bytes to 194 for a zero this array loop"* but can *"halve the execution time"*); *Optimize for size* (`"s"` / `"z"` — `"z"` is the smaller of the two, *"these names were inherited from clang / LLVM and are not too descriptive"*). The chapter's **signature trick** is the **`profile-overrides`** Cargo feature that decouples top-crate optimization from dependencies — `[profile.dev.package."*"] opt-level = "z"` keeps the application at `opt-level = 0` debugger-friendly while shrinking all deps (worked example: `.text` 9060 → 3490 bytes, `.rodata` 1708 → 1100 bytes — **6 KiB Flash saved** with no loss of top-crate debuggability); granular form `[profile.dev.package.cortex-m-rt] opt-level = 0` opts a single dep back out for when stepping into it is needed. Two cross-cutting recommendations: **`[profile.release] debug = true`** because *"for bare metal development, debuginfo is zero cost in the sense that it won't occupy space in Flash / ROM"* — symbols stay host-side in the ELF, never reaching the device — so release builds get to keep their breakpoints. **`"s"` / `"z"` slash LLVM's inline threshold** (75 / 25 vs `2`'s 225 and `3`'s 275, as of `rustc` 1.29.0) — hostile to Rust's many small [[ZeroCostAbstraction|zero-cost-abstraction]] functions (`deref`, `as_ref`, newtype wrappers). Workaround: `rustflags = ["-C", "inline-threshold=225"]` in `.cargo/config.toml`. Caveat on `profile-overrides`: *"generic code can sometimes be optimized alongside the crate where it is instantiated, rather than the crate where it is defined"* — monomorphized code is emitted in the *instantiating* crate, so the override may have no effect on heavy generic structs the top crate uses. Composes with `codegen-units = 1` for *"better optimizations"* on dependencies.
- New pages: **1** — 1 concept ([[OptLevel]] — the `rustc -C opt-level=<N>` dial; full regime table, embedded Flash-budget tension story tying together [[FlashMemory]] / [[ZeroCostAbstraction]] / LLVM inline-threshold, the `profile-overrides` recipe with worked example, the `[profile.release] debug = true` bare-metal recommendation, and the generic-instantiation caveat). No new entities.
- Updated pages: [[TheEmbeddedRustBook]] — added `rust-embedded-book-unsorted-speed-vs-size` to `sources` frontmatter. [[FlashMemory]] — updated **in-place**: added `rust-embedded-book-unsorted-speed-vs-size` to `sources` frontmatter; extended the existing `opt-level = "z"` sentence with a back-reference to [[OptLevel]] as the canonical chapter and a one-line note on `profile-overrides` + `[profile.release] debug = true` (the bare-metal-debuginfo-is-free claim); added [[OptLevel]] and [[rust-embedded-book-unsorted-speed-vs-size]] to connections.

## [2026-05-16] ingest | The Embedded Rust Book — Performing Math Functionality
- Source: `raw/book/src/unsorted/math.md` → `wiki/sources/rust-embedded-book-unsorted-math.md` (file 43/44 of *[[TheEmbeddedRustBook]]* — **second substantive leaf** of the *Unsorted topics* chapter, immediately after [[rust-embedded-book-unsorted-speed-vs-size]] at file 42).
- Content: Short single-recipe chapter. With [[RustStandardLibrary|`std`]] available, math methods (`f32::floor` / `f32::sqrt` / `f32::sin` / `f32::exp`) come for free as **inherent methods** on the primitive float types; under [[NoStd|`#![no_std]`]] these methods are unavailable — [[RustCoreLibrary|`core`]] exposes `f32` / `f64` but **not** their transcendental / rounding operations (the canonical reference implementations live in C's `libm` which `core` cannot depend on). The chapter's drop-in fix is the **[[LibmCrate|`libm`]] crate** — a pure-Rust port of C's `libm` re-exposing the same operations as **free functions** (`use libm::{exp, floorf, sin, sqrtf};` then `sqrtf(x)` replaces `x.sqrt()`) callable from any `no_std` crate without a [[GlobalAllocator|global allocator]] or [[RustRuntime|runtime]]. Worked example rewrites the same four-operation `hello`-style program **twice** — once for hosted [[RustStandardLibrary|`std`]] with `println!`, once for [[ARMCortexM|Cortex-M]] `no_std` ([[CortexMRTCrate|`cortex-m-rt`]] + [[CortexMSemihostingCrate|`cortex-m-semihosting`]] + [[PanicHaltCrate|`panic-halt`]] + [[ARMSemihosting|`hprintln!`]]) — the only syntactic delta is **method-call vs free-function** plus the host-I/O macro swap. **Precision suffix follows C convention**: `f`-suffixed = `f32` (`sqrtf`, `floorf`), no-suffix = `f64` (`sin`, `exp`); the example calls `.into()` on `f32` arguments to widen them. Closes with a **five-crate further-reading list** for when scalar `libm` is not enough — [`cmsis-dsp-sys`](https://github.com/jacobrosenthal/cmsis-dsp-sys) (CMSIS DSP bindings, SIMD-accelerated on [[ARMCortexM|Cortex-M4F]]+), [`constgebra`](https://crates.io/crates/constgebra) (`const fn` linalg), [`micromath`](https://github.com/tarcieri/micromath) (compact `no_std` math), [`microfft`](https://crates.io/crates/microfft) (in-place FFT), [`nalgebra`](https://github.com/dimforge/nalgebra) (general-purpose Rust linalg, optional `no_std`). The five-crate list is mentioned in passing, not given individual pages — `libm` is the scalar-transcendental floor of the stack, the rest layer on top. Re-asserts the standing **"don't `debug::exit()` on hardware"** warning from [[rust-embedded-book-start-semihosting]].
- New pages: **1** — 1 entity ([[LibmCrate]] — `rust-lang/libm`, pure-Rust port of C's `libm`; precision-suffix convention table; `core`/`std`/`libm` gap-filler framing; further-reading mention of the five layered-math crates). No new concepts (no [[FloatingPointUnit]] page — the chapter does not actually discuss FPU; `f32` vs `f64` shows up only implicitly in `libm`'s `f`-suffix API split).
- Updated pages: [[TheEmbeddedRustBook]] — added `rust-embedded-book-unsorted-math` to `sources` frontmatter.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 0 Introduction
- Source: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/introduction.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-0-introduction.md` — opens the new *Dive into Systems* corpus (Matthews / Newhall / Webb).
- Content: Defines a [[ComputerSystem|computer system]] as [[ComputerHardware|hardware]] + [[OperatingSystem|OS]], general-purpose and reprogrammable — pointedly excluding calculators and bare [[Microcontroller|microcontrollers]]. Surveys modern form factors ([[SystemOnAChip|SoCs]], [[SingleBoardComputer|SBCs]] like the [[RaspberryPi]], universally [[MulticoreProcessor|multicore]]). Frames the book's thesis that systems knowledge ([[MemoryHierarchy|memory hierarchy]], [[BinaryRepresentation|binary representation]], OS [[Abstraction|abstractions]]) drives the ability to write efficient code. Previews the book's C-centric, active-reading pedagogy.
- New pages: **17 total** — 1 source ([[dis-0-introduction]]); 6 entities ([[DiveIntoSystems]], [[SuzanneJMatthews]], [[TiaNewhall]], [[KevinCWebb]], [[SwarthmoreCollege]], [[UnitedStatesMilitaryAcademy]]); 10 concepts ([[ComputerSystem]], [[OperatingSystem]], [[ComputerHardware]], [[Abstraction]], [[MulticoreProcessor]], [[SystemOnAChip]], [[SingleBoardComputer]], [[RaspberryPi]], [[CPU]], [[RAM]], [[BinaryRepresentation]], [[ParallelComputing]]) — actually 12 concepts (the count above was wrong; final tally: 1 source + 6 entities + 12 concepts = 19 pages).
- Reuse: [[MemoryHierarchy]] (from [[d2l-computational-performance]]), [[HardwareAbstractionLayer]] (from [[rust-embedded-book-portability-index]]), [[EmbeddedSystems]], [[Microcontroller]], [[DistributedComputing]], [[ZeroCostAbstraction]] — all linked without edit. [[HardwareAbstractionLayer]] specifically called out as the *dual* of [[OperatingSystem]]: the OS-syscall HAL flavor is what the [[DiveIntoSystems]] world inhabits, the trait-HAL flavor is the embedded-`no_std` world of [[TheEmbeddedRustBook]].
- Updated pages: [[wiki/index.md|index.md]] — added 1 source row, 6 entity rows, 12 concept rows; bumped source count 286 → 287.
- Overview: not updated (per instructions — an introduction does not materially shift the synthesis; will revisit when later DIS chapters land).
- Contradictions: none. Definitional complement, not conflict, between [[DiveIntoSystems]]'s OS-required computer-system definition and the no-OS embedded-Rust world.
- Post-validation: all [[wikilinks]] in the new source page point either to newly-created pages above or to pre-existing pages ([[MemoryHierarchy]], [[HardwareAbstractionLayer]], [[EmbeddedSystems]], [[Microcontroller]], [[TheEmbeddedRustBook]], [[ZeroCostAbstraction]], [[STM32F3DISCOVERY]], [[d2l-computational-performance]], [[DataParallelism]], [[ModelParallelism]], [[AutoParallelism]], [[DistributedComputing]]). **Opens the *Dive into Systems* corpus** (Ch 0 of N).

## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch1: Introduction to Parallel Processing
- Source: `raw/parproc-matloff.pdf` (pp. 1–30 of book / PDF pp. 21–50) → `wiki/sources/parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing.md`.
- Chapter 1 of *Programming on Parallel Machines: GPU, Multicore, Clusters and More* by [[NormMatloff]] of [[UCDavis|UC Davis]]. Opens the **parallel-computing textbook corpus**. Sets up the book's full conceptual frame: three motivations (speed / memory / locality), three hardware paradigms ([[SharedMemoryArchitecture]] / [[MessagePassingArchitecture]] / [[SIMD]]), three programmer worldviews ([[Pthreads]] + [[OpenMP]] threads / [[MPI]] message-passing / [[ScatterGather]] manager-worker), demonstrated with worked Sieve-of-Eratosthenes prime finder, matrix-vector multiply, and [[SamplingBucketSort|sampling bucket sort]] examples. Concrete software introduced: [[Pthreads]] (the prime-sieve mutex example — canonical motivation for [[CriticalSection]] and [[Barrier]]), [[OpenMP]] (`#pragma omp parallel/single/for/barrier/critical`), [[MPI]] (pipelined prime finder spread across cluster nodes), [[Snow]] (R scatter/gather over TCP/IP sockets), and [[Rdsm]] (Matloff's own quasi-thread R package built via operator overloading on `[`).
- New pages: **24** total — 8 entities ([[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[Pthreads]], [[OpenMP]], [[MPI]], [[Snow]], [[Rdsm]], [[Beowulf]], plus 2 stubs [[ThinkingMachines]], [[Infiniband]]) and 14 concepts ([[SharedMemoryArchitecture]], [[MessagePassingArchitecture]], [[SIMD]], [[MIMD]], [[SMP]], [[Multicore]], [[Cluster]], [[Barrier]], [[Thread]], [[SamplingBucketSort]], [[ScatterGather]], plus 4 short stubs [[NUMA]], [[CoherentCaches]], [[MapReduce]], [[BucketSort]], [[Process]]).
- Updated pages: [[CriticalSection]] (now covers both the threading-sense — Matloff's mutex-around-`nextbase` example — and the original embedded interrupt-disable sense from the Embedded Rust corpus; `sources` frontmatter += `parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing`); [[ParallelComputing]] (added Matloff's 3×3×3 motivation/architecture/worldview taxonomy as a complementary framing to the *Dive into Systems* baseline; `sources` frontmatter += this chapter; removed a duplicate `ParallelProcessing.md` page I had drafted before discovering [[ParallelComputing]] already existed).
- Reuse: [[CUDA]], [[GPU]], [[Mutex]], [[Rlanguage]] — all referenced without edit (Matloff's mentions add nothing new). [[CriticalSection]] was reused but expanded with a new threading section since the existing content was 100% embedded-only.
- Overview: NOT updated this ingest (Ch1 alone doesn't shift the cross-corpus synthesis; will reassess once a critical mass of ParProcBook chapters has landed).
- Index: source count bumped (+1: this chapter). Entity count +10 (8 first-class new + 2 stubs). Concept count +15 (11 first-class new + 4 stubs). New entries inserted alphabetically; existing list ordering untouched.
- Contradictions: none with existing wiki content. Minor terminological wrinkle worth flagging: Matloff's footnote 1 (p. 3) redefines "processor" to *include* cores ("a dual-core chip will be considered to have two processors"), while the GPU/CUDA wiki pages adopt the more common contemporary convention where "processor" = chip and "core" = execution unit. Both conventions coexist in the literature; Matloff flags his choice explicitly.
- Post-validation: every [[wikilink]] in the new source page resolves to either a newly-created page above or to a pre-existing page ([[CriticalSection]], [[Mutex]], [[CUDA]], [[GPU]], [[Rlanguage]], [[ParallelComputing]]).
- Scope: only Ch1; sibling subagents handle the remaining 13 chapters + 3 appendices of *Programming on Parallel Machines*.

Introduces the [[CompilationProcess|compile-then-run]] cycle and the [[GCC|`gcc`]] command line (`gcc hello.c` / `./a.out` / `-o` / `-Wall` / `-g` / `-lm`). Mandates that every C program defines [[MainFunction|`int main(void)`]] returning `0` for success ([[ExitStatus]]). Establishes the [[VariableDeclaration|`type_name variable_name;`]] *declare-before-use* rule and the [[CPrimitiveType|primitive-type byte-width table]] (`char`/`short`/`int`/`long`/`long long`/`float`/`double` + `unsigned` variants), plus the [[SizeOf|`sizeof`]] operator and the load-bearing `'h'`-char-vs-`"h"`-string distinction. Closes with the [[CArithmeticOperators|arithmetic-operator]] set, flagging the [[IntegerDivision|integer-division truncation]] trap (`11/2 == 5` vs `11/2.0 == 5.5`) and the pre-vs-post [[IncrementOperator|`++` / `--`]] semantic split.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch2: Recurring Performance Issues

- Source: pp. 31–42 of *Programming on Parallel Machines* by [[NormMatloff]] ([[UCDavis]]); short twelve-page chapter installing the vocabulary the rest of the book uses to talk about *why parallel code is slow*.
- New source page: [[parproc-ch02-recurring-performance-issues]].
- New concept pages (10): [[LoadBalancing]], [[CommunicationBottleneck]], [[EmbarrassinglyParallel]], [[WorkStealing]], [[Latency]], [[Bandwidth]], [[StaticTaskAssignment]], [[DynamicTaskAssignment]], [[IterativeAlgorithms]], [[MatrixVectorMultiply]].
- Key takeaways: (1) communication is *always* a potential bottleneck — bus / [[CoherentCaches|cache-coherency]] on shared-memory, network latency on clusters, CPU↔GPU + on-device contention on [[GPU|GPUs]]; (2) the headline thesis — **[[StaticTaskAssignment|static (but possibly randomized) task assignment]] typically beats [[DynamicTaskAssignment|dynamic]]** — justified by the $O(1/\sqrt{m})$ chunk-time concentration argument and an 8000×8000 Mandelbrot timing table (`static` 47.8s, `dynamic` 21.4s, `guided` 29.6s, `random` 15.7s); (3) the meaning of [[EmbarrassinglyParallel|"embarrassingly parallel"]] has *drifted* from "trivially decomposable" to "low [[CommunicationBottleneck|communication]] needs" — the Ch1 prime-finder qualifies as the former but *not* the latter; (4) [[Latency]] and [[Bandwidth]] are two independent dimensions of communication delay, with [[NormMatloff|Matloff]]'s bridge analogy (cars-per-time vs cars-cross-time); (5) the historical [[SharedMemoryArchitecture|shared-memory]]-vs-[[MessagePassingArchitecture|message-passing]] scalability claim is now scrambled by [[GPU]]s demonstrating *"extremely good scalability with shared-memory."*
- Index: bumped Sources 289→290 and Concepts 1056→1066; new entries inserted alphabetically under Sources (after [[parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing]]) and under Concepts.
- Overview: NOT updated per task scope; this chapter expands an existing thread ([[parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing]]) rather than opening a new synthesis axis.
- Contradictions: §2.6 explicitly retracts the historical *"message-passing is more scalable"* community consensus, citing [[GPU]]s — worth flagging for downstream chapters. §2.3.1 explicitly disqualifies the prime-finder from [[parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing]] §1.5.1 as embarrassingly-parallel under the new meaning. The "dynamic-is-better-because-flexible" cultural intuition (implicit in [[Cilk]]-adjacent work-stealing literature) is contradicted by the chapter's math + Mandelbrot timing table.
- Post-validation: all 10 new concept pages exist on disk; the source page's [[wikilinks]] resolve to either the new pages or to pre-existing pages ([[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing]], [[OpenMP]], [[GPU]], [[MPI]], [[Snow]], [[SharedMemoryArchitecture]], [[MessagePassingArchitecture]], [[CoherentCaches]], [[Barrier]], [[CriticalSection]], [[ScatterGather]]); 4 wikilinks point to pages that do not yet exist as files but are referenced from prior wiki content ([[Cilk]], [[Mandelbrot]], [[MemoryAllocation]], [[LatencyHiding]]) — these are valid lazy stubs, intentionally out of scope for this Ch2 ingest.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch3: Shared Memory Parallelism

- Source: pp. 43–80 of *Programming on Parallel Machines* by [[NormMatloff]] ([[UCDavis]]); thirty-eight-page chapter descending from Ch2's vocabulary into the hardware substrate of shared-memory programming.
- New source page: [[parproc-ch03-shared-memory-parallelism]].
- New concept pages (15): [[MemoryInterleaving]], [[BankConflict]], [[Crossbar]], [[OmegaNetwork]], [[TestAndSet]], [[FetchAndAdd]], [[CacheCoherency]], [[MESI]], [[FalseSharing]], [[MemoryConsistency]], [[ProcessorAffinity]], [[SoftwareDSM]], [[JIAJIA]], [[TreeBarrier]], [[ButterflyBarrier]]. Existing pages updated: [[NUMA]] (de-stubbed, Ch3-substantiated), [[Barrier]] (Ch3 §3.12 implementation walkthrough + tree/butterfly), [[CoherentCaches]] (Ch3 §3.5 follow-up added).
- Reused entities (no recreation): [[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[Pthreads]], [[OpenMP]], [[MPI]]. Reused concepts (no recreation): [[LoadBalancing]], [[Latency]], [[Bandwidth]], [[SharedMemoryArchitecture]], [[SMP]], [[NUMA]], [[Multicore]], [[CriticalSection]], [[Barrier]], [[CoherentCaches]].
- Key takeaways: (1) the chapter is a forward-reference resolver — [[CoherentCaches]] / [[NUMA]] / [[Barrier]] from Ch1 and the "memory banks" / "cache-coherency" items deferred by Ch2 §2.8 all get their substantive treatment here; (2) the interconnect scaling table (bus $O(1)/O(1)/O(1)$ vs omega $O(\log n)/O(n)/O(n\log n)$ vs crossbar $O(n)/O(n)/O(n^2)$) is the most reused diagram of the chapter; (3) Matloff carefully separates **[[CacheCoherency|coherency]]** (multi-cache, one address — "do caches agree?") from **[[MemoryConsistency|consistency]]** (one variable — "when does a write become visible?") — they are distinct timing-level problems; (4) [[MESI]] is given in full transition-table detail, including the notable rule that a *write miss does NOT bring the block in*; (5) [[FalseSharing]] is the cache-line-granularity pathology that motivates [[ProcessorAffinity]] and [[JIAJIA]]'s multi-writer feature; (6) the [[JIAJIA]] case study is unusually detailed for a textbook — page-based + scope-consistent + home-based + multi-writer, with the diff/twin reconciliation mechanism and Treadmarks-measured 167 μs twin / 686 μs diff costs; (7) the chapter's three barrier iterations operationalize a recurring [[NormMatloff|Matloff]] pedagogy — *show the obvious bug, then the subtler one, then the correct fix*.
- Index: bumped Sources 290→291; added 15 new Concept entries inserted alphabetically.
- Overview: NOT updated per task scope; this chapter resolves forward-references from Ch1/Ch2 rather than opening a new synthesis axis.
- Contradictions: no contradictions with prior wiki content (this chapter delivers what Ch1/Ch2 promised). One internal-to-the-book tension flagged: §3.8's *"multicore is just SMP"* claim is best read as a software-API statement, not a microarchitectural one — §3.10 ([[ProcessorAffinity]]) and §3.5.3 ([[FalseSharing]]) are tools that matter *more* on multicore than on discrete-CPU SMPs. One historical-accuracy aside: §3.6 claims *"recent Pentium models use Sequential Consistency"* — x86-TSO (a relaxation permitting store-buffer reordering of younger loads past older stores to different addresses) is the documented Intel model since at least the mid-2000s. Not a wiki-internal contradiction.

- Overview: NOT updated per task scope; Ch 1.5 extends the [[CLanguage|C]] surface-area thread incrementally rather than opening a new synthesis axis.
- Contradictions: one **flagged** contradiction-by-design with [[dis-1-4-functions|Ch 1.4]]'s blanket [[PassByValue|pass-by-value]] rule — Ch 1.5 §1.5.3 declares that mutating `a[i]` inside `void test(int a[], int size)` *is* visible to the caller. The chapter frames this as *arrays pass by reference*; the underlying mechanism (array name decays to a pointer to its first element, the *pointer* is what is passed by value) is deferred to Ch 1.6. Recorded in [[PassByReference]] and in the source page's *Contradictions* section as a pedagogical layering, not a wiki-internal inconsistency.

- Overview: NOT updated per task scope; Ch 1.6 closes the Ch 1.5 aggregate-types arc incrementally rather than opening a new synthesis axis.
- Contradictions: one **flagged** layered pedagogical re-contradiction — [[dis-1-4-functions|Ch 1.4]] said "pass by value, always"; [[dis-1-5-arrays-strings|Ch 1.5]] carved out arrays; Ch 1.6 *closes the carve-out* for structs (even structs containing arrays pass by value, because the array-name-decay rule only fires at call boundaries where an array name appears, not a struct value). The wiki's [[PassByValue]] / [[PassByReference]] pages capture this as a multi-chapter layering; not a wiki-internal inconsistency. Also a related lvalue-discipline asymmetry surfaced: [[CArray|array names]] are not [[LValue|lvalues]] (no `arr1 = arr2;`) while [[CStruct|struct variables]] *are* [[LValue|lvalues]] (yes `s1 = s2;`) — wrapping an array in a struct *transitively* promotes the array's assignability. Recorded in [[LValue]] and [[StructAssignment]]; this is a [[CLanguage|C]] language design rule, not a wiki contradiction.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch5: Introduction to GPU Programming with CUDA
- Source: `raw/parproc-matloff.pdf` (book pp. 119–156 = PDF pp. 139–176) → `wiki/sources/parproc-ch05-cuda-gpu-programming.md`.
- Pivot point in the [[NormMatloff]] corpus: Ch1–Ch4 are CPU paradigms (shared-memory + message-passing + SIMD survey; performance vocabulary; shared-memory hardware substrate; OpenMP pragmas). Ch5 introduces the **GPU** as a fourth substrate with [[CUDA]]/[[SIMT]] semantics.
- New concept pages (17): [[SIMT]], [[ThreadDivergence]], [[Warp]], [[Grid]], [[Block]], [[KernelLaunch]], [[GlobalMemory]], [[SharedMemory]], [[ConstantMemory]], [[TextureMemory]], [[UnifiedMemory]], [[MemoryCoalescing]], [[LoopUnrolling]], [[CUBLAS]], [[CUFFT]], [[Thrust]], [[ShortVectors]], [[TrueCaching]], [[StreamingMultiprocessor]], [[StreamingProcessor]], [[OSInHardware]], [[RegisterSpill]], [[AtomicOperation]], [[AtomicAdd]], [[CudaThreadSynchronize]], [[ThreadBarrier]], [[NVIDIAPascal]].
- Updated in place: [[CUDA]] (added Ch5 source + kernel-level section with grid/block/warp hierarchy + memory tier table + synchronization + perf levers + compilation), [[NVIDIA]] (added Tesla/Fermi/Pascal rows to lineage table + Ch5 coverage paragraph), [[GPUMemoryHierarchy]] (added CUDA programmer-view tier nomenclature table cross-referencing each memory page), [[LatencyHiding]] (added Ch5 source + warp-swap-on-stall paragraph + CUDA cross-links).
- Reused (no recreation): [[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing]], [[parproc-ch02-recurring-performance-issues]], [[parproc-ch03-shared-memory-parallelism]], [[parproc-ch04-introduction-to-openmp]], [[GPU]], [[SIMD]], [[SharedMemoryArchitecture]], [[MessagePassingArchitecture]], [[ParallelComputing]], [[Barrier]], [[Multicore]], [[Thread]], [[FalseSharing]], [[MemoryConsistency]], [[CacheCoherency]], [[MemoryInterleaving]], [[BankConflict]], [[LoadBalancing]], [[StaticTaskAssignment]], [[EmbarrassinglyParallel]], [[Latency]], [[Bandwidth]], [[OpenMP]], [[Pthreads]], [[MPI]], [[AtomicClause]].
- Key Ch5 architectural claims: GPUs *run hundreds or thousands of threads at once* vs Ch4's quad-core 4 threads; SM/SP hierarchy = *multi-multiprocessor machine*; warps are 32 threads in SIMT lockstep with [[ThreadDivergence|divergence]] as the within-warp *performance killer*; SM is *an OS in hardware* doing latency hiding by warp-swap on memory stall, with nearly-free context switches because each warp has its own register set → granularity flip: **CUDA wants many small threads, OpenMP wants few large threads**; shared memory is a *programmer-managed cache*; inter-block sync is hard (only atomic ops or host-roundtrip); 128–256 threads/block rule of thumb, must be multiple of 32.
- Headline numerical claim (§5.8): row-major matrix column sums for n=20000 — **CPU 62 s, GPU ~4.5 s** without optimizing coalescing or banks. *No wonder people talk of CUDA in terms like a supercomputer on our desktop.* Immediately disclaimed as embarrassingly parallel.
- Overview: NOT updated per task scope (Ch5 adds a new paradigm — GPU/CUDA — that could justify a synthesis revision, but task says do not modify overview.md).
- Contradictions: none with prior wiki content. Ch5 extends rather than overrides Ch1–Ch4. Tesla-specific numbers (16K shared, 8 banks, 512 threads/block, 786 threads/SM) are flagged as architecture-baseline. Latency-hiding mechanism aligns with Ch2 §2.5's general formulation; coalescing aligns with Ch3 §3.2.1 low-order interleaving; relaxed shared-memory consistency aligns with Ch3 §3.6. [[UnifiedMemory]] does not contradict [[GPU]]'s *transfers are slow* warning — it's explicitly a programmer-convenience feature, not a performance feature.
- Post-validation: all 27 new concept pages exist on disk; the source page's [[wikilinks]] resolve to either the new pages or to pre-existing pages. Index count bumped 292→293.

No prior claim overturned.

Plus `wiki/index.md` (1 source row added after Ch5 row; 11 new concept rows added before `## Syntheses`; count 293 → 294).

- Key Ch6 architectural claims: Thrust *"uses the C++ STL library as a model"* — same source compiles to **CUDA, OpenMP, or Intel TBB** via `-DTHRUST_DEVICE_BACKEND=` (only `device_vector` triggers parallelism, even under the OpenMP back end where "device" is the CPU); a **[[Functor|functor]] is a stateful callable** (struct with `operator()` + member variables) — *"that is what distinguishes functors from function pointers — we can save state"*; **Thrust has no parallel-loop primitive** — emulate via `sequence` + `transform` or via [[CountingIterator|counting iterators]] + `for_each`; **fancy-iterator fusion** via `make_transform_iterator` saves *n* memory reads + *n* memory writes + the eliminated kernel launch; **Thrust calls are sync by default** with only the `transform()`-family async — **inverted from raw CUDA's always-async kernel launches**; **scatter and gather both exist** because of the size-mismatch case (source > destination or vice versa); **counting iterators work with `gather()` not `scatter()`** because only `gather()` takes a `(map_begin, map_end)` pair for the map range.
- Surprise / contradiction-within-chapter: §6.8 motivates fancy iterators as memory-traffic savers; §6.9 measurements show a hand-rolled `for_each` + raw-pointer functor implementation (Code 1) **beats** the Thrust-distribution fancy-iterator implementation (Code 2) — slightly on CUDA (3.67 vs 3.75 s on 10000² GeForce 9800 GTX) but **2-2.4× on OpenMP** (9.57 vs 23.01 s on 6000² at 2 threads). The chapter doesn't reconcile; the implication is fancy-iterator machinery's own per-element overhead can exceed its memory-traffic savings, especially under the OpenMP back end.
- Overview: NOT updated per task scope (Ch6 is a focused deep-dive on a single library introduced in Ch5; the cross-substrate-portability axis was already framed in [[Thrust]]'s Ch5 connection block).
- Contradictions: none with prior wiki content. Ch6 confirms and deepens Ch5's brief Thrust treatment. Naming-clash with Ch1's [[ScatterGather]] (manager-worker pattern) is disambiguated by giving the Thrust permutation primitives their own pages [[ScatterOperation]] / [[GatherOperation]]. The async-`transform`-vs-sync-rest Thrust convention does not contradict Ch5's *all kernel launches are async* — it's a wrapper-library convention layered over the raw-CUDA primitive.
- Post-validation: all 11 new concept pages exist on disk; the source page's [[wikilinks]] resolve to either the new pages or to pre-existing pages. Index count 293 → 294.

- Surprise / framing-update: Ch7 explicitly reframes Ch1's three-paradigms-of-equal-stature posture into a *"message-passing currently dominates"* claim — grounded in cloud computing + Top500 as empirical evidence. (Note: [[Fortran]] / [[Numpy]] / [[MATLAB]] are referenced from the [[RowMajorOrder]] / [[ArrayDecay]] pages and may be forward-reference stubs.)
- Key takeaways: (1) **the array-decay mechanism is the unifying primitive** — `T arr[N]` becomes `T *` at the call boundary, which is what makes pass-by-reference (Ch 1.5) = pass-by-value of a pointer (Ch 2.2) and what makes static and dynamic 1D arrays share a parameter signature (Ch 2.4); Ch 2.5 names it explicitly with the *"C copies the value of the base address to the parameter"* quote; (2) **row-major layout drives the parameter rule** — `&m[i][j] = m_base + i*M + j` needs `M`, hence trailing dimensions are mandatory in 2D parameter declarations; (3) **the two dynamic 2D forms have non-interchangeable parameter types** — single-`malloc` `int *` is *not* `int **`, and a static `int m[N][M]` decays to `int (*)[M]` which is *also* neither — three distinct types with three distinct parameter signatures, despite all three supporting some form of `arr[i][j]` (the single-malloc form being the exception that requires `arr[i*M+j]`); (4) **the syntactic-vs-memory tradeoff** is the Ch 2.5 headline design lesson — `arr[i][j]` ergonomics cost N+1 heap headers + worse cache locality + non-contiguous rows; (5) **ragged 2D requires the array-of-arrays form** — rows of different lengths are inexpressible with a single contiguous malloc; (6) **the lost-bound property of decayed pointers** is the root cause of the corpus's load-bearing security thread (Ch 1.5 [[Strcpy]] / Ch 2.6 [[BufferOverflow]]) — surfaced cleanly on the new [[ArrayDecay]] page.
- Overview: NOT updated per task scope. Ch 2.5 is the *completion* of the array-story arc Ch 1.5 / Ch 2.2 / Ch 2.4 already framed at the overview level — the synthesis-layer claim "C arrays are pointer-to-first-element under the hood" is already implicit; Ch 2.5 makes it nameable but does not introduce a new synthesis-level claim.
- Contradictions: none. Ch 2.5 *completes* [[dis-1-5-arrays-strings|Ch 1.5]]'s multidimensional-array deferral, *ratifies* [[dis-2-4-dynamic-memory|Ch 2.4]]'s static-vs-dynamic 1D-array unification, and *grounds* [[dis-1-5-arrays-strings|Ch 1.5]]'s [[PassByReference|pass-by-reference]] exception in the [[ArrayDecay|array-decay]] mechanism that [[dis-2-2-pointers|Ch 2.2]] previously reconciled at the value-vs-reference level. No prior claim overturned.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch8: Introduction to MPI
- Source: `raw/parproc-matloff.pdf` (book pp. 187–212 = PDF pp. 207–232) → `wiki/sources/parproc-ch08-introduction-to-mpi.md`. The dedicated [[MPI]] chapter of *Programming on Parallel Machines* by [[NormMatloff]] of [[UCDavis]] — *"MPI is the de facto standard for message-passing software."*
- Expanded entity page: [[MPI]] — substantially grown from 2.3K stub to ~12K full API surface; added the SPMD execution model, the MPICH/LAM/Open-MPI/MVAPICH/Intel-MPI implementation taxonomy, the 7-collective API table with the 12-op reduce vocabulary, the four-call communicator-subdivision recipe, the buffering/synchrony/deadlock layering, the nonblocking variants, the `MPI_Sendrecv_replace` safe-exchange call, and the bindings-in-other-languages tour. Frontmatter `sources` extended with `parproc-ch07-message-passing-systems` and `parproc-ch08-introduction-to-mpi`.
- New concept pages (14): [[SPMD]] (Single Program Multiple Data — MPI's execution model; distinct from [[SIMD]] / MPMD), [[CollectiveCommunication]] (the all-nodes-participate primitive class; *"all the nodes in the group execute this line"* clarification + the hardware-multicast performance caveat from Ch7), [[MPISend]] (six-argument signature + the *"safe to overwrite the send buffer"* return semantic + the endianness/word-width heterogeneity-broker rationale for typed messages), [[MPIRecv]] (the matching seven-arg receive + `MPI_ANY_SOURCE` / `MPI_ANY_TAG` wildcards + `MPI_Get_count` for variable-length receives + the OS→app buffer-copy cost that RDMA eliminates), [[MPIBcast]] (one-to-all broadcast + the *all-nodes-execute-the-collective* pedagogical clarification), [[MPIReduce]] (all-to-one reduce + the 12-op table + `MPI_MINLOC` / `MPI_MAXLOC` pair-type semantics), [[MPIAllreduce]] (reduce+bcast collapsed; the [[AllReduce]] / [[RingAllReduce]] / [[NCCL]] / [[Horovod]] connection), [[MPIGather]] (concatenating all-to-one + the *"fifth argument is redundant with the second"* API wart), [[MPIAllgather]] (gather with all-destinations; recursive-doubling on a logical [[Hypercube]] achieves bandwidth-optimal `(P-1)/P × |total|` traffic per rank), [[MPIScatter]] (one-to-all chunked distribution; canonical opener for the [[ScatterGather]] manager/worker pattern), [[MPIBarrier]] (sole-argument barrier; *"explicit barriers are less common in message-passing programs than in the shared-memory world"*), [[MPICommunicator]] (group + context; `MPI_COMM_WORLD` default + the four-call sub-communicator recipe + virtual-topology APIs), [[BufferingMPI]] (the three-layer user-program / MPI-internals / TCP-stack abstraction + the TCP-coalescing trap + the exact MPI-standard send-buffer-reusability semantic + the synchronous tag-mismatch [[Deadlock]] pattern + the *"asynchronous = unsafe"* terminology), [[NonblockingComm]] (`MPI_Isend` / `MPI_Irecv` / `MPI_Wait` / `MPI_Test` / `MPI_Probe`; the "living dangerously" safety contract).
- Updated index.md: 1 new source row (Ch8 inserted between Ch7 and Ch5 — chapter-order respected); 14 new concept rows before `## Syntheses`; count 295 → 296. The pre-existing index history comment block was NOT modified per task scope.
- Reused (no recreation): [[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing]], [[parproc-ch04-introduction-to-openmp]], [[parproc-ch07-message-passing-systems]], [[DijkstraAlgorithm]], [[Hypercube]], [[NetworkOfWorkstations]], [[Cluster]], [[Beowulf]], [[MessagePassingArchitecture]], [[Infiniband]], [[Latency]], [[Bandwidth]], [[EmbarrassinglyParallel]], [[OpenMP]], [[ScatterGather]], [[MapReduce]], [[Snow]], [[Deadlock]], [[GDB]], [[Barrier]], [[OmegaNetwork]], [[Broadcasting]], [[AllReduce]], [[RingAllReduce]], [[Horovod]], [[NCCL]], [[ScatterOperation]], [[GatherOperation]] — referenced without edit.
- Key takeaways: (1) **MPI is an API standard, not an implementation** — MPICH (active, network/platform tailoring), LAM (discontinued → Open MPI), Open MPI (active, *"not to be confused with [[OpenMP]]"*), MVAPICH ([[Infiniband]]-tuned), Intel MPI; (2) **[[SPMD]] is the canonical execution model** — every node runs the same compiled binary, branching by rank; (3) **collectives subsume hand-coded send/recv loops** — the §8.6.1 *Refined Dijkstra* deletes three functions and replaces them with three collective calls; (4) **the 12-op reduce table** — `MPI_MAX` / `MPI_MIN` / `MPI_SUM` / `MPI_PROD` / `MPI_LAND` / `MPI_LOR` / `MPI_LXOR` / `MPI_BAND` / `MPI_BOR` / `MPI_BXOR` / `MPI_MAXLOC` / `MPI_MINLOC`, the `*LOC` variants on `MPI_2INT` pair type; (5) **the exact MPI-standard send semantic is buffer-reusability** — *"`MPI_Send(x,...)` will return only when it is safe for the application program to write over the array which it is using to store its message, i.e. x"* — explicitly NOT *"receiver has the data"*; (6) **synchronous communication can deadlock on tag mismatch**, *"asynchronous communication is considered unsafe in MPI terminology"* but most code accepts the buffer-exhaustion risk for the performance; (7) **[[NonblockingComm|`MPI_Isend` / `MPI_Irecv`]] enable comm/compute overlap** but require the *"don't touch the buffer until completion"* contract; (8) **[[MPICommunicator|communicators]] subdivide the world into groups via the four-call `MPI_Comm_group` → `MPI_Group_incl` → `MPI_Comm_create` → `MPI_Group_rank` recipe**; (9) **debug MPI by attaching GDB** to running processes via `while (dbg) ;` + `gdb a.out <PID>` + `set var dbg = 0`; (10) **typed messages broker heterogeneity** — Intel little-endian vs Sun SPARC big-endian, 32-vs-64-bit word width — *"some of the machines literally receive the data backwards!"* if raw bytes were sent.
- Overview: NOT updated per task scope. Ch8 is the API-detail expansion of the [[MPI]] forward-reference Ch1 made and the hardware-substrate Ch7 backfilled — no new synthesis-level paradigm shift beyond the *"message-passing currently dominates"* claim Ch7 already established.
- Contradictions: none with prior wiki content. Ch8 *adds API surface* to [[MPI]] (Ch1 only introduced send/recv); *operationalizes* the Ch7 *"MPI_Bcast is not magic without hardware support"* warning (§8.6.2's rationale for using collectives over send loops); *reuses* the [[DijkstraAlgorithm]] worked example Ch4 developed under [[OpenMP]]. The §8.1.2 `processes`-vs-`nodes` terminology disambiguation is a clarification, not a contradiction. The §8.3.3.1 implementation-dependent behavior of `MPI_Init` *deepens* (not overturns) Ch1's introduction.
- Surprise / framing-update: the chapter is uncharacteristically deep on **the gap between API semantics and implementation cost** — §8.6.2's *"using the broadcast may improve performance"* (conditional on hardware multicast + matching MPI build), §8.7.1's TCP-stream-coalescing trap + the exact buffer-reusability standard wording, §8.7.2's classification of async communication as *"unsafe"* in MPI terminology even though *"most people are willing to go ahead with their 'unsafe' code."* Programming MPI well requires understanding what `MPI_Send` *does not* guarantee.
- Post-validation: all 14 new concept pages exist on disk; [[MPI]] entity page substantially expanded (sources frontmatter extended); the source page's [[wikilinks]] resolve to either the 14 new pages, the expanded entity, or pre-existing pages ([[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing]], [[parproc-ch02-recurring-performance-issues]], [[parproc-ch04-introduction-to-openmp]], [[parproc-ch07-message-passing-systems]], [[DijkstraAlgorithm]], [[Hypercube]], [[NetworkOfWorkstations]], [[Cluster]], [[MessagePassingArchitecture]], [[Infiniband]], [[Latency]], [[Bandwidth]], [[EmbarrassinglyParallel]], [[OpenMP]], [[ScatterGather]], [[Deadlock]], [[GDB]], [[MapReduce]], [[Snow]]). ## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch9: MapReduce Computation
- Source: `raw/parproc-matloff.pdf` (book pp. 213–222 = PDF pp. 233–242) → `wiki/sources/parproc-ch09-mapreduce-computation.md`. Ch9 of *Programming on Parallel Machines* by [[NormMatloff]] of [[UCDavis]] — the dedicated [[MapReduce]] chapter covering Hadoop, HDFS, streaming, and the Snowdoop alternative.
- Expanded concept page: [[MapReduce]] — grown from 935-byte stub to full reference covering map/shuffle/reduce phases, JobTracker/TaskTracker terminology, HDFS role, streaming protocol, locality scheduling, and comparison with Snowdoop. Frontmatter `sources` extended with `parproc-ch09-mapreduce-computation`.
- Updated concept page: [[KMeansClustering]] — added Snowdoop parallel implementation section (§9.4.2); frontmatter `sources` extended.
- New entity page (1): [[Hadoop]] — Apache Hadoop, the dominant MapReduce framework; Java-based with streaming interface; HDFS for distributed storage; limitations for iterative algorithms.
- New concept pages (5): [[HDFS]] (Hadoop Distributed File System — ≥3-replica fault tolerance, data locality scheduling, *"Moving computation is cheaper than moving data"*), [[HadoopStreaming]] (stdin/stdout language-agnostic interface — `key \t data` format, combiner optimization), [[WordCount]] (canonical MapReduce "Hello World" — R streaming implementation + Snowdoop implementation), [[Snowdoop]] (Matloff's pure-R MapReduce alternative — `clusterCall` + `addlists` + `Reduce`, free in-memory persistence for iterative algorithms, no fault tolerance), [[MapReduce]] (expanded from stub).
- Index updated: 1 new source row (Ch9 inserted before Ch8 — chapter order respected); 1 new entity row ([[Hadoop]]); 5 new concept rows; count 296 → 297.
- Reused (no recreation): [[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing]], [[parproc-ch07-message-passing-systems]], [[parproc-ch08-introduction-to-mpi]], [[MapReduce]] (expanded), [[Snow]], [[ScatterGather]], [[Cluster]], [[NetworkOfWorkstations]], [[MPIScatter]], [[MPIGather]], [[ApacheSpark]], [[KMeansClustering]] (updated).
- Key takeaways: (1) **MapReduce = scatter/gather + sorting** — the shuffle phase is what distinguishes MapReduce from a plain scatter/gather; (2) **HDFS data locality** — map tasks scheduled on the node holding their input block; *"Moving computation is cheaper than moving data"*; (3) **combiner reduces network traffic** — pre-aggregates mapper output on the mapper node before the shuffle hop; (4) **Hadoop's core limitation is disk-bound iteration** — *"one cannot keep intermediate results in memory between Hadoop runs"* — iterative algorithms pay re-read cost every pass; (5) **Snowdoop trades fault tolerance for R-nativeness** — no Java, no HDFS, no configuration; uses Snow's `clusterCall` + standard `Reduce`; (6) **Snowdoop's k-means is faster for iterative algorithms** — data persists in worker RAM across iterations, *"here it comes for free, no special effort needed"*; (7) **Snowdoop's parallel I/O requires physically distributed chunks** — *"neither Hadoop, Spark nor Snowdoop will achieve full parallel reading if the file chunks are all on the same disk"*.
- Contradictions: none with prior wiki content. Ch9 consistently extends the MapReduce/Snow/scatter-gather thread from Ch1 and Ch7. The Snowdoop description of [[KMeansClustering]] is additive to the ISLR sequential description.
- Post-validation: all 5 new concept pages and 1 new entity page exist on disk; [[MapReduce]] and [[KMeansClustering]] updated in-place; source page [[wikilinks]] resolve to existing pages ([[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[parproc-ch01-intro-parallel-processing]], [[parproc-ch07-message-passing-systems]], [[parproc-ch08-introduction-to-mpi]], [[MapReduce]], [[Hadoop]], [[HDFS]], [[HadoopStreaming]], [[WordCount]], [[Snowdoop]], [[KMeansClustering]], [[Snow]], [[ScatterGather]], [[Cluster]], [[NetworkOfWorkstations]]). Index count 296 → 297. **ParProcBook corpus: 9/N chapters ingested (Ch1, Ch2, Ch3, Ch4, Ch5, Ch6, Ch7, Ch8, Ch9).**

## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch10: The Parallel Prefix Problem
- Source: `raw/parproc-matloff.pdf` (book pp. 223–234 = PDF pp. 243–254) → `wiki/sources/parproc-ch10-parallel-prefix-problem.md`. Ch10 of *Programming on Parallel Machines* by [[NormMatloff]] — the dedicated [[PrefixScan]] / parallel prefix chapter.
- Expanded concept page: [[PrefixScan]] — grown from a Ch5/Ch6 stub to a full reference covering inclusive vs exclusive scan, Hillis-Steele algorithm, Blelloch algorithm, blocked n > p strategy, complexity comparison table, platform support table, and applications catalogue. Frontmatter `sources` extended with `parproc-ch10-parallel-prefix-problem`; `tags` extended with `openmp`.
- New concept pages (6): [[HillisSteeleScan]] ($\log_2 n$-round data-parallel algorithm; $O(n \log n)$ work; red/black double-buffer), [[BlellochScan]] (work-efficient two-pass up-sweep/down-sweep; $O(n)$ work), [[RunLengthEncoding]] (count/value compression; parallel decompression via exclusive prefix scan of run-counts; OpenMP + Thrust implementations), [[MovingAverage]] (sliding-window mean; parallel computation via `exclusive_scan` + `transform`; Rth/Thrust code), [[LambdaFunction]] (C++11 anonymous function syntax; `[=]`/`[&]` capture modes; replaces functor structs in Thrust), [[StreamCompaction]] (filter/pack primitive; predicate → scan → scatter pattern; `thrust::copy_if`).
- Index updated: 1 new source row (Ch10 inserted before Ch9 — chapter order respected); 6 new concept rows before `## Syntheses`; count 297 → 298.
- Reused (no recreation): [[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[OpenMP]], [[Thrust]], [[Block]], [[Warp]], [[parproc-ch05-cuda-gpu-programming]], [[parproc-ch06-thrust-programming]], [[parproc-ch09-mapreduce-computation]], [[Functor]], [[ParallelComputing]].
- Key takeaways: (1) **Hillis-Steele is $O(n \log n)$ total work** — not optimal; the existing [[PrefixScan]] page implied $O(\log n)$ depth without calling out the work cost; the expansion adds nuance. (2) **Blocked strategy is the production approach** — the three-phase serial-per-chunk / parallel-scan-of-sums / add-offsets-back plan in §10.2 matches §5.11's CUDA approach exactly. (3) **Prefix scan powers at least three non-obvious applications** — run-length decompression, moving average, and stream compaction all reduce to a scan + simple per-element work. (4) **C++11 lambdas are strictly cleaner than functor structs** for Thrust — `[=](double& a, double& b){ return (a-b)/wa; }` replaces a 10-line struct; requires `-std=c++11`.
- Contradictions: none with prior wiki content. The "O(log n)-depth" claim in the prior [[PrefixScan]] page was not wrong (depth is correct) but was incomplete (total work is O(n log n) for Hillis-Steele); the expanded page now distinguishes depth from work. The chapter does not name the Blelloch algorithm; [[BlellochScan]] page is created for completeness from the broader literature.
- Post-validation: all 6 new concept pages exist on disk; [[PrefixScan]] updated in-place; source page [[wikilinks]] resolve to existing pages ([[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[PrefixScan]], [[HillisSteeleScan]], [[BlellochScan]], [[RunLengthEncoding]], [[MovingAverage]], [[LambdaFunction]], [[OpenMP]], [[Thrust]], [[Block]], [[Warp]], [[StreamCompaction]], [[parproc-ch05-cuda-gpu-programming]], [[parproc-ch06-thrust-programming]], [[parproc-ch09-mapreduce-computation]]). ## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch12: Introduction to Parallel Sorting
- Source: `raw/parproc-matloff.pdf` (book pp. 257–272 = PDF pp. 277–292) → `wiki/sources/parproc-ch12-parallel-sorting.md`. Ch12 of *Programming on Parallel Machines* by [[NormMatloff]] — dedicated parallel sorting chapter covering all three paradigms (shared memory, message passing, GPU).
- New source page: [[parproc-ch12-parallel-sorting]] — 7 algorithms across §12.1–12.7: [[Quicksort]] (separation process + `separate()` correctness, OpenMP `nowait` variant, [[Hyperquicksort]]), [[MergeSort]] (sequential, shared-memory, binary-tree message-passing), [[CompareExchange|compare-exchange/compare-split]] primitive, [[BitonicMergesort]] (bitonic sequence definition, `sortbitonic()` recursion, general-sequence construction), [[BubbleSort]] (parallel inner loop), [[OddEvenTransposition]] (odd/even phased compare-exchanges, CUDA implementation with per-iteration kernel launch and pointer-swapping), [[Shearsort]] (2D mesh, snakelike output, `MPI_Alltoall` for column ops), [[SamplingBucketSort]] (MPI decile-splitter implementation), [[RadixSort]] (CUDPP segmented scan), [[EnumerationSort]] (rank-count, trivially parallel outer loop).
- New concept pages (9): [[Hyperquicksort]] (d-cube algorithm, power-of-2 constraint, deadlock avoidance with `MPI_SendRcv`), [[MergeSort]] (sequential pseudocode, shared-memory, binary-tree message-passing with chunk-size trade-off), [[CompareExchange]] (pool-and-split primitive; role in bitonic/hyperquicksort/odd-even), [[BitonicMergesort]] (bitonic sequence definition + pairwise split proof + general construction algorithm), [[BubbleSort]] (O(n²) serial, parallel inner loop, chunking), [[OddEvenTransposition]] (odd/even phase pseudocode, CUDA one-iteration-per-kernel implementation with `daaux` scratch array), [[Shearsort]] (ceil(log₂n)+1 phase algorithm, 2×2 example, MPI implementation note), [[RadixSort]] (bit-level bucket sort, CUDPP/segmented-scan implementation), [[EnumerationSort]] (rank-count pseudocode, outer- and inner-loop parallelism).
- Updated concept pages (4): [[Quicksort]] — added Ch12 separation-process detail, `separate()` correctness, OpenMP `nowait` variant, prefix-scan alternative, hyperquicksort extension; `sources` frontmatter extended. [[SamplingBucketSort]] — added §12.5 MPI decile-splitter implementation; `tags` and `sources` frontmatter extended. [[BucketSort]] — added [[RadixSort]] connection row; Ch12 source noted. [[Quicksort]] index row updated to reflect Ch12 extensions.
- Index updated: 1 new source row (Ch12 inserted after Ch11 — chapter order respected); 11 new concept rows (BitonicMergesort, BubbleSort, Compare-Exchange, EnumerationSort, Hyperquicksort, Mergesort, Odd-Even Transposition Sort, Radix Sort, Shearsort inserted at alphabetical positions; Quicksort and Sampling Bucket Sort rows updated in-place).
- Reused (no recreation): [[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[OpenMP]], [[CUDA]], [[MPI]], [[PrefixScan]], [[Hypercube]], [[LoadBalancing]], [[parproc-ch04-introduction-to-openmp]], [[parproc-ch07-message-passing-systems]], [[parproc-ch10-parallel-prefix-problem]].
- Key takeaways: (1) **Separation correctness is the algorithmic heart of quicksort** — `separate()` places the pivot in its final position; the sub-ranges never escape their index bounds; prefix-scan provides an alternative parallel separation. (2) **Hyperquicksort = quicksort + hypercube structure** — median broadcast + d rounds of compare-exchange on a d-cube; result is globally sorted distributed chunks without a gather step. (3) **Compare-exchange is the universal parallel sorting primitive** — bitonic mergesort, hyperquicksort, odd-even transposition, and bubble sort all reduce to variants of compare-exchange. (4) **Bubble sort is "much-maligned" serially but usable in parallel** — the inner loop is data-parallel; odd-even transposition is the regularized parallel variant. (5) **CUDA odd-even requires one kernel launch per iteration** — separate blocks cannot synchronize across a launch; pointer-swapping between primary and scratch arrays avoids write hazards. (6) **Shearsort is the canonical 2D-mesh algorithm** — snakelike output; column ops require matrix transpose via `MPI_Alltoall`. (7) **Radix sort = bucket sort with no sampling** — bit-level buckets; uniformity assumption replaces sampling; CUDPP implements via segmented scan.
- Contradictions: none with prior wiki content. Bubble sort reframe ("much-maligned serially, usable in parallel") is a new perspective not contradicting any prior page. Sampling bucket sort MPI implementation is consistent with the Ch1 OpenMP version. **ParProcBook corpus: 12/N chapters ingested (Ch1–Ch12).**
- Post-validation: all 9 new concept pages exist on disk; 4 concept pages updated in-place; source page [[wikilinks]] resolve to existing pages ([[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[Quicksort]], [[Hyperquicksort]], [[MergeSort]], [[BitonicMergesort]], [[CompareExchange]], [[BubbleSort]], [[OddEvenTransposition]], [[Shearsort]], [[SamplingBucketSort]], [[RadixSort]], [[EnumerationSort]], [[OpenMP]], [[CUDA]], [[MPI]], [[PrefixScan]], [[parproc-ch04-introduction-to-openmp]], [[parproc-ch07-message-passing-systems]], [[parproc-ch10-parallel-prefix-problem]]). ## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch13: Parallel Computation for Audio and Image Processing

Source: `raw/parproc-matloff.pdf` pp. 273–290 (PDF pp. 293–310). Chapter 13 of *Programming on Parallel Machines* by [[NormMatloff]].

- Source page created: `wiki/sources/parproc-ch13-audio-image-processing.md`.
- Concept pages created (5 new): [[DiscreteFourierTransform]], [[FastFourierTransform]], [[ImageSmoothing]], [[EdgeDetection]], [[FFTW]].
- Concept pages updated (2): [[FourierSeries]] (stub upgraded to full page), [[CUFFT]] (added inverse-transform, 2-D, and FFTW cross-references).
- Index updated: 1 new source row (Ch13 inserted after Ch12, chapter-order respected); 7 concept rows added/updated (DiscreteFourierTransform, EdgeDetection, FastFourierTransform, FFTW, FourierSeries description, ImageSmoothing); count bumped 297 → 298.
- Reused (no recreation): [[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[CUDA]], [[OpenMP]], [[MPI]], [[Quicksort]], [[Hyperquicksort]], [[MatrixMultiplication]], [[parproc-ch05-cuda-gpu-programming]], [[parproc-ch11-parallel-matrix-operations]], [[parproc-ch12-parallel-sorting]], [[parproc-ch04-introduction-to-openmp]].
- Key takeaways: (1) **DFT is a Vandermonde matrix-vector product** — C = (1/n)AX; inversion exact via A(q)⁻¹ = (1/n)A(1/q); 2-D DFT separates into row+column 1-D DFTs. (2) **FFT reduces DFT from O(n²) to O(n log n)** via Cooley-Tukey divide-and-conquer; two parallel strategies: FFT recursion (mirrors Quicksort/Hyperquicksort from Ch12) vs matrix-multiply approach (preferred in shared memory). (3) **Image smoothing = low-pass filter; edge detection = high-pass filter** — both follow the same forward-DFT → coefficient zeroing → inverse-DFT pipeline. (4) **FFTW is the CPU-parallel counterpart to CUFFT** — provides OpenMP and MPI callable versions; R's fft() is serial but parApply enables 2-D parallelism. (5) **Normalization hazard** — R's `fft()` is "unnormalized"; users mixing CUFFT, FFTW, and R must reconcile 1/n vs 1/√n vs no-factor conventions.
- Contradictions: none with prior wiki content. Minor note: §13.3.2 qualifies divide-and-conquer as problematic in shared memory (thread starvation) — absent from Ch12's parallel Quicksort; consistent given FFT's shallower log₂ n recursion depth.
- **ParProcBook corpus: 13/N chapters ingested (Ch1–Ch13).**
- Post-validation: all 4 new concept pages exist on disk; 3 existing concept pages updated ([[SwitchStatement]], [[CaseLabel]], [[Typedef]]); source page `[[wikilinks]]` resolve to either the 4 new pages, the 3 updated pages, or pre-existing pages (full reuse list above). ## [2026-05-17] ingest | ParProcBook Ch14: Parallel Computation in Statistics/Data Mining

Source: `raw/parproc-matloff.pdf` pp. 291–304 (PDF pp. 311–324). Chapter 14 of *Programming on Parallel Machines* by [[NormMatloff]].

- Source page created: `wiki/sources/parproc-ch14-statistics-data-mining.md`.
- Concept pages created (5 new): [[AprioriAlgorithm]], [[ItemsetAnalysis]], [[MarketBasketProblem]], [[KernelDensityEstimation]], [[Histogram]], [[MonteCarloSimulation]].
- Concept pages updated (2): [[KMeansClustering]] (Ch14 Snow implementation with Manhattan distance added; sources frontmatter extended), [[PrincipalComponentAnalysis]] (parallel PCA via Ch11 eigenvector methods added; sources frontmatter extended).
- Index updated: 1 new source row (Ch14 inserted after Ch13); 6 concept rows added (AprioriAlgorithm, Histogram, ItemsetAnalysis, KernelDensityEstimation, MarketBasketProblem, MonteCarloSimulation).
- Reused (no recreation): [[NormMatloff]], [[UCDavis]], [[Snow]], [[OpenMP]], [[CUDA]], [[MPI]], [[FastFourierTransform]], [[ProbabilityDensityFunction]], [[BankConflict]], [[parproc-ch13-audio-image-processing]], [[parproc-ch11-parallel-matrix-operations]], [[parproc-ch09-mapreduce-computation]], [[parproc-ch05-cuda-gpu-programming]], [[KMeansClustering]], [[PrincipalComponentAnalysis]].
- Key takeaways: (1) **Apriori pruning is monotone** — support decreases under set extension, so infrequent itemsets can be pruned; inner loops embarrassingly parallel, but refinements (hash trees, large F_i) make coordination harder. (2) **KDE has convolution form** — f̂(t) = (1/nh)Σk((t−Xᵢ)/h) reduces to parallel FFT (Ch13), connecting density estimation to frequency-domain work. (3) **CUDA histograms are a bank-conflict problem** — Podlozhnyuk resolves it via per-thread (histogram64) or per-warp (histogram256) subhistograms in shared memory, merged with atomicAdd. (4) **K-means Ch14 uses Manhattan distance** — distinct from Ch9's Euclidean/Snowdoop implementation; same algorithm, different distance and framework. (5) **Monte Carlo is not trivially parallel** — C's random() gives correlated or identical streams per thread; CURAND/RngStream/SPRNG/OpenMP-MT are required.
- Contradictions: none with prior wiki content. Notable: Ch14's k-means (Snow, Manhattan distance) and Ch9's k-means (Snowdoop, Euclidean distance) are two distinct parallel implementations of the same algorithm — not contradictory, but users should not conflate them.
- **ParProcBook corpus: 14/N chapters ingested (Ch1–Ch14).**
- Post-validation: all 5 new concept pages exist on disk; 2 existing concept pages updated; all [[wikilinks]] in source page resolve to existing pages or newly created pages; 6 concept rows + 1 source row added to index.md.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 2.9.3 The void* Type
- Source: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C2-C_depth/advanced_voidstar.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-2-9-3-voidstar.md`. **Third subsection of [[dis-2-9-advanced|Ch 2.9]]** of *[[DiveIntoSystems]]*; finally delivers the [[VoidPointer|`void *`]] type-erased pointer that [[dis-2-2-pointers|Ch 2.2]] named-and-deferred and that [[dis-2-4-dynamic-memory|Ch 2.4]] silently relied on as [[Malloc|`malloc`]]'s return type.
- New concept pages (4): [[VoidPointer]] (the type-erased pointer with the size-uniformity rationale and the two canonical use cases: [[Malloc|`malloc`]] return type, [[Pthreads|pthread]] thread-main args), [[TypeCast]] (the `(T *)e` recast mechanism with the double-parens `*((T *)vp)` idiom), [[GenericPointer]] (the design pattern naming the *type-erased programming* approach with three canonical uses — generic return type / generic parameter / generic storage), [[Memcpy]] (`void *memcpy(void *dest, const void *src, size_t n)` named-and-deferred as a `void *`-bearing routine; three rules — no overlap, caller owns dest size, byte-level no type interpretation).
- Resolves the [[dis-2-2-pointers|Ch 2.2]] *"`void *` deferred to later Ch 2 sections"* promise. Explains the mechanism Ch 2.4's [[Malloc|`malloc`]] signature exposed without justification.
- Headline rule: **size-uniformity** — all pointer variables have the same width on a given platform (4 bytes on 32-bit, 8 on 64-bit), so the compiler can allocate `void *` storage without knowing the pointee type. The pointee's `sizeof` is irrelevant to the pointer-variable's storage size.
- Headline discipline: **recast before dereference** — `*vp` is a type error; programmer must supply `(type *)vp` first; idiomatic form is `*((int *)args)` with double parens.

- Reused (no recreation): [[DiveIntoSystems]], [[SuzanneJMatthews]], [[TiaNewhall]], [[KevinCWebb]], [[CLanguage]], [[Pointer]], [[PointerType]], [[CMemoryAddress]], [[VoidType]], [[Malloc]], [[DereferenceOperator]], [[NullPointer]], [[ConstQualifier]], [[BufferOverflow]], [[Strcpy]], [[Strncpy]], [[Strlcpy]], [[StructAssignment]], [[SizeOf]], [[SizeT]], [[dis-2-2-pointers]], [[dis-2-4-dynamic-memory]], [[dis-2-6-strings]], [[dis-2-9-advanced]], [[dis-2-9-2-cmd-line-args]].

These will be created when the relevant chapters (Ch 9–10 concurrency, Ch 2.9.4 pointer arithmetic) are ingested.
- Key takeaways: (1) **`void *` works because pointer width is uniform** — the architecture fixes pointer-variable storage size; pointee type only matters when interpreting the address; (2) **the cast is information the programmer supplies** — `void *` carries no pointee metadata, so the recast is the programmer's *"trust me, this is what's at that address"* assertion to the compiler; (3) **two canonical use cases** in Ch 2.9.3 — `malloc` (generic *return*) and pthread thread main (generic *parameter*); a third — generic *storage* in containers — is implicit in broader corpus; (4) **the bargain** is compile-time type-checking ↔ runtime flexibility; (5) **`void` vs `void *`** are fundamentally different — `void` is a non-type, `void *` is a real pointer type. The `*` changes everything.
- Contradictions: none. Ch 2.9.3 is purely additive — it *explains* the mechanism [[dis-2-4-dynamic-memory|Ch 2.4]]'s [[Malloc|`malloc`]] signature already exposed, and resolves the `void *` deferral [[dis-2-2-pointers|Ch 2.2]] flagged. The modern-C *"cast is implicit"* note on [[Malloc|`malloc`]] is consistent with the existing [[Malloc]] page's note that *"in modern C the `void *` assigns to any pointer type without an explicit cast (older code shows `p = (int *) malloc(...)`)"*.
- Overview: NOT updated per task scope.
- Post-validation: all 4 new concept pages exist on disk; source page [[wikilinks]] resolve to existing pages (10 pre-existing concepts + 4 new pages) or intentional forward-reference stubs (8 stubs, none load-bearing); index updated with 1 source row + 4 concept rows; entity [[DiveIntoSystems]] corpus 20 → 21 chapters. **DiveIntoSystems corpus: 21 chapters ingested — Ch 1 complete + Ch 2.1 + 2.2 + 2.3 + 2.4 + 2.5 + 2.6 + 2.7 + 2.8 + 2.9 (hub) + 2.9.1 + 2.9.2 + 2.9.3.**

- Contradictions: none. RowMajorOrder and MemoryAllocation pages are additive (new source + gcc tip); no conflict with any existing content.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 4.1 Number Bases and Unsigned Integers
- Source: `sources/dis-4-1-bases.md` ([[DiveIntoSystems]] Ch 4.1 — **opens Ch 4 *Binary and Data Representation***, the book's first chapter below [[CLanguage|C]] into bit-level encoding).
- URL: https://diveintosystems.org/book/C4-Binary/bases.html
- Authors: [[SuzanneJMatthews|Matthews]] / [[TiaNewhall|Newhall]] / [[KevinCWebb|Webb]].
- New concept pages: **6** — [[NumberBase]] (positional-system framework $\sum d_i \cdot B^i$; the unifying abstraction across decimal/binary/octal/hex; *"the position of each digit in the number determines how important that digit is to the overall value"*), [[BinaryNumber]] (base $B=2$, digits $\{0,1\}$; the native form of data inside [[ComputerHardware|hardware]]; worked examples `0b1000=8`, `0b10110100=180`), [[HexadecimalNumber]] (base $B=16$, digits $\{0\text{–}9,A\text{–}F\}$; one hex digit = 4 bits, the canonical compact display form for binary data; worked example `0x23C8=9160`), [[OctalNumber]] (base $B=8$, digits $\{0\text{–}7\}$; one octal digit = 3 bits; historically dominant on PDP-era machines, surviving today in Unix `chmod` permissions and the leading-`0` numeric-literal footgun), [[UnsignedInteger]] (non-negative-only [[BinaryNumber|binary]] encoding; $N$-bit cell stores $2^N$ distinct values over $[0,\,2^N-1]$; 8-bit `unsigned char` → `0`–`255`), [[BasePrefix]] ([[CLanguage|C]]-source-syntax convention `0b` / `0` / `0o` / `0x` that disambiguates a literal's base — source-syntax convenience only, all four `0xFF`/`0b11111111`/`0377`/`255` compile to the same byte; the bare-`0` octal form is a notorious footgun where `0755` is octal `493`).

One's complement appears as an intermediate step in the [[TwosComplement|two's-complement]] negation algorithm ("flip all bits" produces the one's complement; adding 1 then gives two's complement) but is not given its own treatment. A future chapter or wiki-only treatment may fill this gap.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 4.6 Bitwise Operators

- Source: https://diveintosystems.org/book/C4-Binary/bitwise.html — [[dis-4-6-bitwise]] — the **operator-surface section** of [[DiveIntoSystems]] Ch 4 *Binary and Data Representation* that exposes [[CLanguage|C]]'s six [[BitwiseOperator|bitwise operators]] (`&`, `|`, `^`, `~`, `<<`, `>>`) as the per-bit instantiation of [[LogicGate|logic gates]].
- Headline thesis: *"bitwise operators directly apply logic gate behavior to bit sequences"* — they are the per-bit primitives executing in one clock cycle with no carry propagation, the cheapest ops in the [[InstructionSetArchitecture|ISA]]. Bridges Ch 4.1–4.5's value-level integer arithmetic into the **bit-manipulation idioms** real systems code uses (bit-test / set / clear / toggle / mask / pack).
- Six operators codified: [[BitwiseAnd|`&`]] (masking — `26 & 54 = 18`); [[BitwiseOr|`|`]] (bit-setting — `26 | 54 = 62`); [[BitwiseXor|`^`]] (toggling / equality-test — `26 ^ 54 = 44`); [[BitwiseNot|`~`]] (bit-flip — `~26 = -27` because *"bitwise NOT only flips the bits and doesn't add one"* — the `~x = -x - 1` identity under [[TwosComplement|two's complement]]); **left shift `<<`** (zero-fill, truncate at MSB — `13 << 3 = 104`, multiplication by $2^N$); **right shift `>>`** — split by operand type: [[LogicalRightShift|logical]] (unsigned → zero-fill — `0xFF000000 >> 12 = 0x000FF000`) vs. [[ArithmeticRightShift|arithmetic]] (signed → replicate [[SignBit|sign bit]] — `0xFF000000 >> 12 = 0xFFFFF000`), *"the C compiler automatically selects the appropriate shifting variant based on variable declaration."*
- Headline category warning: *"programmers often confuse the bitwise `&&` and `||` operators with `&` and `|` operators"* — the [[LogicalOperator|logical operators]] short-circuit on truth values, the bitwise ones act per bit on the operand width.
- **Closes the [[dis-4-4-3-mult-div|Ch 4.4.3]]-deferred *bit-shift power-of-two shortcut*** for multiplication and division (`x * 16 = x << 4`; `x / 16 = x >> 4` unsigned) — the **strength-reduction** pattern compilers apply automatically; survives in hand-tuned code on platforms with slow `MUL` / `DIV` (e.g., [[ARMCortexM|Cortex-M0]] has no hardware divide).
- **2 new concept pages**: [[BitwiseOperator]] (umbrella for `&` / `|` / `^` / `~` with truth tables, canonical idioms, and hardware mapping to x86 `AND` / `OR` / `XOR` / `NOT` and ARM `AND` / `ORR` / `EOR` / `MVN`) + [[BitShift]] (umbrella for `<<` / `>>` with the [[LogicalRightShift|logical]] / [[ArithmeticRightShift|arithmetic]] right-shift split, the power-of-two multiplication / division shortcut, and the [[UndefinedBehavior|UB]] pitfalls — shift-by-≥-width, signed-shift-overflow, negative shift count).
- [[DiveIntoSystems]] entity in-place update: corpus 42 → **43 chapters**; sources list appended `dis-4-6-bitwise`; Connections section gained Ch 4.6 bullet above Ch 4.5.
- Index: added 1 source row above the [[dis-4-1-bases|Ch 4.1]] entry; added an `### Added with [[dis-4-6-bitwise]]` concept block above the [[dis-4-5-overflow|Ch 4.5]] one.

- Source: https://diveintosystems.org/book/C4-Binary/byte_order.html → [[dis-4-7-byte-order]].
- Ch 4.7 *Integer Byte Order* — **closes Ch 4 *Binary and Data Representation***. The **memory-layout section** codifying [[ByteOrder|byte order]] / [[Endianness|endianness]] as the convention by which a multibyte value's [[Byte|bytes]] are laid out across consecutive [[MemoryAddress|memory addresses]]. *"Byte order, sometimes referred to as endianness, describes the order in which the bytes of a multibyte value are ordered in memory."* Single-byte values have no endianness — the question is only meaningful for values wider than one byte.
- Two conventions codified: **[[BigEndian|big-endian]]** (MSByte at lowest address — matches written-numeral left-to-right reading order; [[PowerPC]] / [[SPARC]] / [[Motorola68k]] / mainframes / network protocols / [[JavaVirtualMachine|JVM]] internals) and **[[LittleEndian|little-endian]]** (LSByte at lowest address — reversed from written numerals; [[X86]] / [[X86_64]] / [[ARM]] / [[RISCV|RISC-V]] — universal in modern deployed CPUs as of 2025).
- Detection demonstrated via the [[CCast|`char *` cast]] over `int value = 0xAABBCCDD;` walking `p[0..3]` — on x86 / x86-64 the bytes walk out `DD CC BB AA`, proof of little-endian. The `%02hhX` format specifier renders a `signed char` as exactly two hex digits.
- **Three canonical scenarios** where endianness leaks out of the [[Abstraction|abstraction]]: (1) **network transmission** — *"the IETF defines big-endian as the [[NetworkByteOrder|network byte order]]"*, the standardization that fixes cross-host [[TCPIP|TCP/IP]] / [[UDP]] serialization; [[POSIX]] supplies [[Htonl|`htonl` / `ntohl` / `htons` / `ntohs`]] (no-ops on big-endian hosts, real byte-swaps on little-endian); (2) **binary file formats** — written on one host and read on another with different endianness yields garbage unless the format pins endianness ([[JPEG]] big, [[BMP]] / [[WAV]] / [[RIFF]] little, [[TIFF]] declares per-file via `II` / `MM` magic) or includes a [[ByteOrderMark|BOM]] ([[Unicode|UTF-16 / UTF-32]]); (3) **debugger / raw-memory inspection** — `gdb`'s [[GdbExamineMemory|`x/4xb`]], `od -tx1`, `hexdump -C` show **raw byte sequence** which reads reversed on little-endian hosts vs. the value the program holds.
- Architecture map: [[X86]] always little-endian (since [[Intel8080]] 1974); [[ARM]] / [[MIPS]] / [[PowerPC]] are technically [[BiEndian|bi-endian]] but virtually always run little-endian in deployment ([[POWER8]]+ on Linux flipped to little-endian to ease x86 porting). [[IBMZArchitecture|IBM z/Architecture]] mainframes are the last major big-endian deployment outside network protocol code paths.
- Etymology: Jonathan Swift's *[[GulliversTravels|Gulliver's Travels]]* (1726) via [[DannyCohen|Danny Cohen]]'s 1980 [[IETF]] IEN-137 memo *On Holy Wars and a Plea for Peace* — *"choose a convention and commit."*
- **4 new concept pages** (per *"Bundle if appropriate"*): [[ByteOrder]] (umbrella with full treatment), [[Endianness]] (alias-with-disambiguation pointing at [[ByteOrder]]), [[BigEndian]] (the MSByte-first convention), [[LittleEndian]] (the LSByte-first convention). The umbrella + alias + two sub-conventions structure mirrors how [[BitShift]] / [[LogicalRightShift]] / [[ArithmeticRightShift]] would naturally split — but here all four merit standalone pages because the cross-corpus references ([[NetworkByteOrder]], [[ByteSwap]], file formats, etc.) name them individually.
- Entity update: [[DiveIntoSystems]] — added Ch 4.7 row to Connections; prepended Ch 4.7 paragraph to Corpus status; bumped `chapters_ingested` 43 → 44; added `dis-4-7-byte-order` to frontmatter sources list.
