- Overview: skipped per task scope.
- Stubs left for later: [[NetworkByteOrder]] / [[ByteSwap]] / [[BSWAP]] / [[Htonl]] / [[ByteOrderMark]] / [[BiEndian]] / [[DannyCohen]] / [[GulliversTravels]] / [[IETF]] / [[X86_64]] / [[ARMv7]] / [[ARMv8]] / [[AArch64]] / [[POWER8]] / [[IBMZArchitecture]] / [[IBM360]] / [[Alpha]] / [[VAX]] / [[PDP11]] / [[Motorola68k]] / [[SPARC]] / [[PowerPC]] / [[RISCV]] / [[RISCV64]] / [[MIPS]] / [[Intel8080]] / [[ARMCortexM|CortexM]] / [[Aliasing]] / [[CompileTimeEndianDetection]] / [[SerialProtocol]] / [[I2C]] / [[SPI]] / [[Ethernet]] / [[JPEG]] / [[BMP]] / [[PNG]] / [[TIFF]] / [[WAV]] / [[ZIP]] / [[ELF]] / [[PE]] / [[RIFF]] / [[UTF16]] / [[UTF32]] / [[Unicode]] — cross-referenced architecture / format / protocol stubs the [[ByteOrder]] / [[BigEndian]] / [[LittleEndian]] pages mention but don't materialize.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 5.7 Pipelining Instruction Execution

- Source: `wiki/sources/dis-5-7-pipelining.md` (URL: https://diveintosystems.org/book/C5-Arch/pipelining.html).
- Extends [[dis-5-6-instruction-execution|Ch 5.6]]'s sequential four-stage [[FetchDecodeExecuteCycle|fetch-decode-execute]] cycle into an **overlapped [[InstructionPipelining|pipeline]]** — once full the CPU completes **one instruction per [[ClockCycle|cycle]]** (effective [[CyclesPerInstruction|CPI]] → 1) **without reducing per-instruction latency**. Throughput-not-latency optimization. Five-stage Fetch-Decode-Execute-Memory-WriteBack for loads/stores. ARM 3+ stages; Intel Core i7 14 stages.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | DSPy Programming Overview

- Ingested https://dspy.ai/learn/programming/overview/ → `wiki/sources/dspy-programming-overview.md` (slug `dspy-programming-overview`). **Page 2 of 13** of the [[DSPy]] *Learn* documentation corpus — opens the **Programming** stage of the three-stage Programming → Evaluation → Optimization model framed by [[dspy-learn-index|the Learn index]]. Raw markdown saved to `raw/dspy-programming-overview.md` (extracted via WebFetch).
- **Framework-level thesis surfaced**: ***"DSPy is a bet on writing code instead of strings. In other words, building the right control flow is crucial."*** This is the canonical statement of DSPy's design philosophy and is now the load-bearing quote on both [[DSPy]] and the new [[DSPyProgrammingModel]] concept page.
- **Four-concerns decomposition**: the Programming Overview names the four orthogonal concerns a "conventional prompt" entangles into one non-portable string — **signature** (typed I/O), **adapter** (formatting / parsing), **module** logic (strategy, e.g. "thinking step by step" or tool use), and **manual optimization** (trial-and-error) — and maps each onto a DSPy API artifact ([[DSPySignatures]] / [[DSPyAdapters]] / [[DSPyModules]] / [[DSPyOptimizers]]). This is the page where the mapping is *defined*; it underwrites every later sub-page in the Learn section.
- **Three portability claims** committed to by the page: (1) swap LM / adapter without changing the rest of the program; (2) swap one module for another (`dspy.ChainOfThought` ↔ `dspy.ProgramOfThought`) without modifying the Signature — Signature is the **stable interface**, Module the **swappable strategy**; (3) run prompt-optimization *or* weight fine-tuning against the same program (the both-axes optimizer story already noted on [[DSPy]] and [[dspy-learn-index]]).
- **Programming-stage operationalization**: three nested sub-steps — define task → define initial pipeline → craft a handful of examples — under a "**start simple, then grow**" discipline. The recommended starting point for any new task is a single `dspy.ChainOfThought` module, with complexity added incrementally based on observation.
- 1 new concept page: [[DSPyProgrammingModel]] (DSPy's separation-of-concerns design philosophy; captures the four-concerns decomposition, the three portability claims, the start-simple-then-grow discipline, and the framework's relation to [[PromptEngineering]] / [[2604.25850-agentic-harness-engineering|harness engineering]] / [[LLMModuloFramework|LLM-Modulo]] / [[AgenticAI]] DAGs). The dedicated Signatures / Modules / Adapters / Tools / Optimizers / MCP / Language-Models concept pages are **deferred to sibling ingests** of the corresponding Learn sub-pages — per CLAUDE.md's *be conservative — prefer wikilinks over creating premature stubs* directive.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | DSPy Language Models

- Ingested https://dspy.ai/learn/programming/language_models/ → `wiki/sources/dspy-language-models.md` (slug `dspy-language-models`). **Page 3 of 13** of the [[DSPy]] *Learn* documentation corpus — operationalizes [[dspy-programming-overview|the Programming Overview's]] *"swap the LM without changing the rest of your logic"* portability claim into a **two-line code change**. Raw markdown saved to `raw/dspy-language-models.md` (extracted via WebFetch).
- **The `dspy.LM` universal client surfaced.** Single class wraps every supported provider behind one initializer signature — `dspy.LM('provider/model-name', api_key=..., **kwargs)` — bound to the rest of the program via thread-safe `dspy.configure(lm=...)` (global) or `with dspy.context(lm=...):` (block-local). This is the framework's *concrete* answer to the portability claim — the swap is two lines, not a refactor.
- **Upstream dependency exposed: [[LiteLLM]].** The page is explicit: *"DSPy supports dozens of LLM providers via LiteLLM."* `dspy.LM` is therefore a thin facade — provider authentication / request shaping / response parsing is LiteLLM's job; DSPy adds four framework-level capabilities on top (caching default-on, `rollout_id` deterministic cache-busting, per-call `lm.history` telemetry, `model_type="responses"` Responses-API opt-in). This is a useful refinement of the wiki's DSPy framing — the LM-agnosticism story is *grounded in LiteLLM's provider abstraction*, not freshly invented by DSPy.
- **Three deployment regimes with the same API.** Managed ([[openai|OpenAI]] / [[anthropic|Anthropic]] / [[gemini|Gemini]] / Vertex AI / [[Databricks]] / [[Anyscale]] / [[TogetherAI|Together AI]] / [[microsoft|Azure]]) / self-hosted GPU ([[SGLang]] via OpenAI-compatible endpoint) / local laptop ([[Ollama]] via `ollama_chat/...`). The OpenAI-compatible-endpoint pattern (prefix `openai/`, set `api_base`) is the catch-all escape hatch.
- **`rollout_id` reconciles reproducibility-by-default with deliberate variance.** Caching is on by default → repeated calls return identical outputs. `rollout_id=N` is part of the cache key → same `(rollout_id, temperature)` is cached but different `rollout_id` at `temperature > 0` yields a different sample. Currently supported by OpenAI / Databricks. This is the wiki's first record of a framework solving the *reproducibility-vs-sampling* tension explicitly.
- **`lm.history` is the per-call telemetry surface optimizers will replay against.** Every call records `prompt / messages / kwargs / response / outputs / usage / cost / timestamp / uuid / model / response_model / model_type` — wire-frame for cost accounting, debugging, and [[DSPyOptimizers|Optimizer]] replay.
- 1 new concept page: [[DSPyLM]] (DSPy's universal LM-client abstraction; `provider/model-name` convention; thread-safe global/block-local bind modes; thin facade over [[LiteLLM]] with four framework-level capabilities on top; spans three deployment regimes with the same API; the concrete operationalization of [[DSPyProgrammingModel|the Programming Model's]] *swap-the-LM* portability claim).
- 4 new entity pages: [[LiteLLM]] (BerriAI's open-source Python provider-abstraction SDK; the upstream dependency the page names explicitly), [[Ollama]] (local-laptop LLM runtime), [[SGLang]] (self-hosted-GPU inference server explicitly recommended by the page for *"accurate open models"*), [[TogetherAI]] (managed-API LLM inference provider — `together_ai/...` model string).
- [[DSPy]] entity page **updated in-place** with a new *"LM-agnosticism in practice: the `dspy.LM` client"* section (full deployment-regime table + LiteLLM dependency disclosure + four framework-level capabilities), a new `dspy-language-models` source in the frontmatter `sources:` list, a new contribution row, and 9 new connection rows.
- `wiki/index.md`: new source row inserted above the [[dspy-programming-overview]] row at the top of Sources; new [[DSPyLM]] concept row inserted above the [[DSPyProgrammingModel]] row under Concepts; 4 new entity rows ([[LiteLLM]] / [[Ollama]] / [[SGLang]] / [[TogetherAI]]) inserted between [[LishaLi]] and [[SyneTune]] under Entities (grouped together rather than scattered alphabetically — they form a coherent DSPy-Language-Models-page cluster); source-count comment bumped 305 → 306; concept-count comment bumped 1098 → 1099; entity-count comment bumped 311 → 315; DSPy entity row updated to reference the new client + LiteLLM + deployment-regime story.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | DSPy Signatures

- Ingested https://dspy.ai/learn/programming/signatures/ → `wiki/sources/dspy-signatures.md` (slug `dspy-signatures`). **Page 4 of 13** of the [[DSPy]] *Learn* documentation corpus — **defines** the first of the four orthogonal artifacts [[DSPyProgrammingModel|the Programming Model]] factors out of a conventional prompt: the **Signature**. Raw markdown saved to `raw/dspy-signatures.md` (extracted via WebFetch).
- **The Signature abstraction defined.** *"A signature is a declarative specification of input/output behavior of a DSPy module"* — runtime program object, not documentation convention; field names carry semantic weight (*"a `question` differs fundamentally from an `answer`"*); the stable interface that survives [[DSPyModules|Module]] / [[DSPyAdapters|Adapter]] / [[DSPyLM|LM]] swaps.
- **Two equivalent surface forms.** **Inline** strings (`"question -> answer"`, `"context: list[str], question: str -> answer: str"` for RAG QA, `"question, choices: list[str] -> reasoning: str, selection: int"` for multi-output) optionally paired with `instructions=` kwarg for top-level guidance; and **class-based** (`class Emotion(dspy.Signature)`) which carries three things the inline form cannot — task-description docstring, `dspy.InputField(desc=...)` input hints, `dspy.OutputField(desc=...)` output constraints.
- **Five-tier type system.** Basic Python (`str` / `int` / `bool`), `typing` composites (`list[str]` / `dict[str, int]` / `Optional[float]` / `Union[str, int]`), arbitrary pydantic models (`class QueryResult(pydantic.BaseModel)` → `dspy.Signature("query: str -> result: QueryResult")`), dot-notation nested types (`MyContainer.Query`), DSPy-special types (`dspy.Image` for multi-modal input via `dspy.Image.from_url(...)`, `dspy.History` for conversational context). `typing.Literal[...]` named as the DSPy-idiomatic closed-set-classification primitive (the `Emotion` example).
- **Type-checking is warn-not-fail.** Input field values validated against type annotations at call time; mismatches trigger warnings, not exceptions; can be disabled globally via `dspy.configure(warn_on_type_mismatch=False)`. Consistent with DSPy's prototyping-first discipline.
- **Modules expand signatures under the hood.** Most consequential implementation note: *"Many DSPy modules expand signatures under the hood. For example, `dspy.ChainOfThought` adds a `reasoning` field."* A user-declared `'document -> summary'` signature passed to `dspy.ChainOfThought` acquires a `response.reasoning` slot the user never declared — the mechanism that licenses [[DSPyProgrammingModel|the Programming Model's]] *"swap one module for another without modifying the signature"* portability claim.
- **Five worked examples on the page.** (A) `'sentence -> sentiment: bool'` → `dspy.Predict` (inline, bool output); (B) `'document -> summary'` → `dspy.ChainOfThought` (signature expanded with `reasoning`); (C) `class Emotion(dspy.Signature)` with `Literal[...]` → `dspy.Predict` (closed-set classification); (D) `class CheckCitationFaithfulness(dspy.Signature)` with `desc=` hints + `dict[str, list[str]]` output → `dspy.ChainOfThought` (worked instance of a Signature acting as a verifier / metric); (E) `class DogPictureSignature(dspy.Signature)` with `image_1: dspy.Image` → `dspy.Predict` (multi-modal input).
- **Closing framing claim.** *"While signatures streamline prototyping with structured inputs/outputs, their primary value emerges when composing multiple signatures into larger DSPy modules and compiling these into optimized prompts and finetunings."* Positions Signatures as the **stable interface layer** that [[DSPyModules|Modules]] compose and [[DSPyOptimizers|Optimizers]] tune against — the API-level form of [[DSPyProgrammingModel|the Programming Model's]] *Signature-is-the-stable-interface, Module-is-the-swappable-strategy* point.
- 1 new concept page: [[DSPySignatures]] (DSPy's declarative input/output specification; inline / class-based duality; five-tier type system; warn-not-fail type checking; modules expand signatures under the hood; the **stable interface** the rest of DSPy composes against; **closes the long-standing forward reference** carried by [[DSPy]] / [[DSPyProgrammingModel]] / [[DSPyLM]] / [[dspy-learn-index]] / [[dspy-programming-overview]] / [[dspy-language-models]] since the DSPy corpus opened on 2026-05-17).
- 0 new entity pages.
- [[DSPy]] entity page **updated in-place**: `sources:` frontmatter appended `dspy-signatures`; the [[DSPySignatures|Signatures]] bullet in the *Core abstractions* section expanded with the inline / class-based duality, five-tier type system, the signature-expansion mechanism, and the *generate prompts rather than asking the user to write them* framing; new contribution row for [[dspy-signatures]] added under *Tracked contributions* (Page 4 of 13).

Headline rule: same [[CmpInstruction|`cmp`]] sets all four flags; the signed/unsigned split lives in the **consumer** mnemonic (`g`/`l` consume SF + OF; `a`/`b` consume CF).

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 7.4.2 If Statements in Assembly (x86-64)

- Source: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C7-x86_64/if_statements.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-7-4-2-x86-64-if-statements.md`.
- **68th ingested DIS chapter** — second leaf of [[dis-7-4-x86-64-conditional-loops|Ch 7.4]] *Conditional Control and Loops (x86-64)*, after [[dis-7-4-1-x86-64-preliminaries|Ch 7.4.1]] *Preliminaries* and before Ch 7.4.3 *Loops*.
- Coverage: operationalizes the [[X86FlagsRegister|FLAGS]] / [[CmpInstruction|`cmp`]] / [[X86JumpInstructions|conditional-jump]] vocabulary from [[dis-7-4-1-x86-64-preliminaries|Ch 7.4.1]] into the **canonical if-then-else compilation pattern** via a single worked `getSmallest(int x, int y)` example traced through (1) **unoptimized branch-based** assembly (`cmp` + `jle` + then-branch fall-through + `jmp` to join + labeled else-branch — diamond with source condition **negated** so the jump skips the then-branch) and (2) **`-O1`-optimized branch-free** assembly (`cmp` + tentative `mov` of one arm + [[X86ConditionalMove|`cmovle`]] overwrite — three branchless instructions, `%rip` never redirected). Headline rules: *"a standard if statement [is] where a jump occurs when conditions are **not** true"*; *"when both branches of an if-else statement simply assign different values to a single variable, the compiler may use `cmov` to remove the branch entirely"*; *"the compiler takes the safe road and uses jumps"* whenever pre-evaluating both arms could fault or have side effects ([[NullPointerSafety|null-pointer]] caveat). The `cmov` family mirrors the [[X86JumpInstructions|conditional-jump family]] one-to-one (twelve named variants — signed `cmove`/`cmovne`/`cmovs`/`cmovns`/`cmovg`/`cmovge`/`cmovl`/`cmovle`; unsigned `cmova`/`cmovae`/`cmovb`/`cmovbe`).
- New concept pages (3): [[X86ConditionalMove]] (branch-free conditional-assignment primitive); [[AsmIfThenElse]] (canonical if-then-else compilation pattern in both Form 1 branch-based and Form 2 branch-free); [[BranchInstruction]] (generic control-flow-transfer primitive — **promoted from forward-reference**; resolves references from [[dis-7-4-x86-64-conditional-loops|Ch 7.4]]'s *"branch-driven non-sequential execution"* and [[dis-5-8-pipelining-advanced|Ch 5.8]]'s [[ControlHazard|control-hazard]] / [[BranchPrediction|branch-prediction]] story; covers both jump and `cmov` realizations + cross-ISA branch-vs-predicated split).
- `wiki/index.md`: new source row inserted above the [[dis-7-4-1-x86-64-preliminaries]] row at the top of Sources; new *Added with [[dis-7-4-2-x86-64-if-statements]]* subsection added under Concepts with the three new concept rows (above the [[dis-7-4-1-x86-64-preliminaries]] subsection).
- `wiki/entities/DiveIntoSystems.md`: `chapters_ingested` bumped 67 → 68; `sources` frontmatter list appended with `dis-7-4-2-x86-64-if-statements`; new top connection row added for [[dis-7-4-2-x86-64-if-statements]] above [[dis-7-4-1-x86-64-preliminaries]].
- Overview update **skipped** per request — pattern-delivery leaf, no new synthesis material.
- Forward references introduced (broken-on-purpose): [[NullPointerSafety]] / [[SegmentationFault]] / [[TernaryOperator]] / [[CompilerOptimization]]. Deliberate stubs that safety / language-feature / optimization-theme chapters will mint.
- Validation: all `[[wikilink]]`s in the new source page, three new concept pages, and in-place index/entity edits resolve to either an existing wiki page or to the deliberate forward references above. No contradictions — Ch 7.4.2 **operationalizes** [[dis-7-4-1-x86-64-preliminaries|Ch 7.4.1]]'s mechanism vocabulary into a compilation pattern, **confirms** [[dis-5-8-pipelining-advanced|Ch 5.8]]'s [[BranchPrediction|branch-prediction]] / [[ControlHazard|control-hazard]] motivation by showing `cmov` as the compiler-side **elimination** response, and **confirms** [[dis-7-3-x86-64-arithmetic|Ch 7.3]]'s [[CompilerOptimization|strength-reduction]] theme by adding a third concrete `-O1` payoff at the control-flow surface ([[X86ShiftInstructions|`shl`-as-multiply]] / [[LeaInstruction|`lea`-as-arithmetic]] / **`cmov`-as-if-else**).

- Coverage: closes the per-ISA tour by adding the **function-call instruction family** ([[CallInstruction|`callq`]] / [[RetInstruction|`retq`]] / [[LeaveInstruction|`leaveq`]]) and operationalizing the **System V AMD64 [[CallingConvention|calling convention]]** at the [[X86_64|x86-64]] assembly level. Six argument registers (`%rdi`/`%rsi`/`%rdx`/`%rcx`/`%r8`/`%r9` for params 1–6, stack for 7+); `%rax` for return value; [[StackPointer|`%rsp`]] *"always points to the top of the stack"*; [[FramePointer|`%rbp`]] *"points to the base of the current stack frame"*; [[CallerSavedRegister|caller-saved]] (`%rax`/`%rcx`/`%rdx`/`%rsi`/`%rdi`/`%r8`–`%r11`) vs [[CalleeSavedRegister|callee-saved]] (`%rbx`/`%rbp`/`%r12`–`%r15`) partition. Canonical prologue (`push %rbp; mov %rsp, %rbp; sub $N, %rsp`) / epilogue (`leaveq; retq`) pattern formalized — [[LeaveInstruction|`leaveq`]] = *"equivalent to `mov %rbp, %rsp` then `pop %rbp`"*.
- New concept pages (6): [[CallInstruction]], [[RetInstruction]], [[LeaveInstruction]], [[CallingConvention]], [[CallerSavedRegister]], [[CalleeSavedRegister]].
- **Forward-reference closures (2)**: [[StackPointer]] and [[FramePointer]] — both first flagged in [[dis-7-2-x86-64-common|Ch 7.2]]'s ingest log (line "Forward references introduced: ... [[StackPointer]], [[FramePointer]] ... — will resolve as Ch 7 progresses"). Now first-class concept pages with the full `%rsp` / `%rbp` invariants, prologue/epilogue lifecycle, frame-chain semantics, and System V ABI alignment / preservation contract.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | DSPy MCP

- Source: `https://dspy.ai/learn/programming/mcp/` → `raw/dspy-mcp.md` → `wiki/sources/dspy-mcp.md`.
- **Page 8 of 13** of the [[DSPy]] *Learn* documentation; defines DSPy's integration with the **[[ModelContextProtocol|Model Context Protocol (MCP)]]** — [[anthropic|Anthropic]]'s open protocol for connecting LMs to external tools via standalone servers.
- Coverage: the **separation-of-responsibility framing** (*"DSPy doesn't manage MCP server connections directly. Instead, you connect to an MCP server using the `mcp` library, then convert the server's tools into DSPy tools with `dspy.Tool.from_mcp_tool`."*); the **single class-method bridge** `dspy.Tool.from_mcp_tool(session, tool)` that converts an MCP tool descriptor (from `await session.list_tools()`) into a [[DSPyTools|`dspy.Tool`]] instance whose execution callback routes asynchronously back through the open `mcp.ClientSession`; **two transports** — stdio (`stdio_client` + `StdioServerParameters` for local server processes, no network exposure) and streamable HTTP (`streamablehttp_client` for remote endpoints, bidirectional streaming); the **byte-identical conversion code** across transports once the `ClientSession` is open; the **MCP async-end-to-end constraint** ([[react|`dspy.ReAct`]] must be invoked via `.acall(...)`, the agent invocation must live inside the `async with` session block, the toolset is fixed for the lifetime of the session); installation via `pip install -U "dspy[mcp]"`; the three MCP motivations (use standardized tools / share tools across stacks / simplify integration).
- New concept pages (2): [[ModelContextProtocol]] (framework-agnostic protocol page — the [[anthropic|Anthropic]]-authored open standard; two-transport story; four-step client lifecycle: open transport → `ClientSession + initialize` → `list_tools/resources/prompts` → invoke via `call_tool`; the load-bearing **decoupling-of-definition-from-consumption** property; **resolves the long-standing forward reference** carried by [[DSPy]] / [[DSPyTools]] / [[dspy-learn-index]] / [[dspy-tools]] since the DSPy corpus opened on 2026-05-17) and [[DSPyMCP]] (DSPy-specific binding — lighter-weight, treats `dspy.Tool.from_mcp_tool(...)` as the first-class abstraction; documents the three operational rules — `.acall(...)`, agent-inside-session, toolset-fixed-per-session — and the four-concerns-decomposition composition story). The split between [[ModelContextProtocol]] and [[DSPyMCP]] mirrors the [[LiteLLM]] / [[DSPyLM]] precedent of protocol/library upstream + framework-binding downstream.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | DSPy Evaluation Overview

- Source: `https://dspy.ai/learn/evaluation/overview/` → `raw/dspy-evaluation-overview.md` → `wiki/sources/dspy-evaluation-overview.md`.
- **Page 9 of 13** of the [[DSPy]] *Learn* documentation; **opens the Evaluation stage** of the [[dspy-learn-index|three-stage Programming → Evaluation → Optimization]] model — the first page outside the Programming stage. The Programming stage (pages 2–8) defined *how to express* a DSPy program ([[DSPySignatures|Signatures]] / [[DSPyModules|Modules]] / [[DSPyAdapters|Adapters]] / [[DSPyTools|Tools]] / [[ModelContextProtocol|MCP]] under the [[DSPyProgrammingModel|Programming Model]]); this page defines *how to measure* one before any [[DSPyOptimizers|optimization]] is attempted.
- Coverage: the **four-step iterative-evaluation loop** — (1) **collect a development set** of 20–200 worked examples — *"Even 20 input examples of your task can be useful, though 200 goes a long way"* — from four ranked sourcing options (adjacent public datasets like [[HuggingFace]] or StackExchange → permissive-license re-use → manual labeling → deployed-demo collection); (2) **define a DSPy metric** as a callable `(example, prediction) -> score` — scalar `accuracy` for simple tasks, a *"smaller DSPy program that checks multiple properties of the output"* for long-form tasks (DSPy's structural operationalization of the [[llmasjudge|LLM-as-judge]] pattern); (3) **run development evaluations** to produce a **numerical baseline** + a **qualitative diagnostic** — *"This will probably allow you to spot any major issues, and it will define a baseline for your next steps"* — the diagnostic may force a return to Programming rather than proceed forward; (4) **(recursive) optimize the metric itself** when the metric is a DSPy program — *"If your metric is itself a DSPy program, a powerful way to iterate is to optimize your metric itself. That's usually easy because the output of the metric is usually a simple value (e.g., a score out of 5)"* — a recursive-self-improvement claim structurally novel relative to other LLM-evaluation frameworks the wiki has recorded.
- Three load-bearing commitments distinguish DSPy Evaluation from generic ML evaluation: **small dev sets are sufficient** (20-example floor lowers start-up cost; 200-example ceiling marks the *"goes a long way"* point of diminishing returns for dev-set-scale work — Evaluation is **diagnostic**, not training); **intermediate-step labels are almost never needed** (*"You almost never need labels for the intermediate steps in your program in DSPy"* — the metric scores **final outputs only**; intermediate-step quality, e.g. the `reasoning` from [[ChainOfThought|`dspy.ChainOfThought`]] or the `trajectory` from [[react|`dspy.ReAct`]], is the [[DSPyOptimizers|Optimizer's]] job to discover — DSPy's structural answer to the **intermediate-supervision problem** in chain-of-thought training); **the metric is itself iterative** (*"Invest in defining metrics and improving them incrementally over time; it's hard to consistently improve what you aren't able to define"* — the [[DSPyProgrammingModel|"start simple, then grow"]] discipline from page 2 restated at the metric layer; the metric has versions over the project's lifetime).
- New concept pages (1): [[DSPyEvaluation]] — the canonical anchor for DSPy's evaluation philosophy; documents the four-step loop, the three load-bearing commitments, the recursive-self-improvement claim (Step 4), the *what Evaluation produces* three-artifact framing (development set + metric + baseline score, which are the **interface contract** between the Programming and Optimization stages), and the position-in-the-wiki against [[ModelEvaluation]] (general) / [[OfflineEvaluation]] / [[OnlineEvaluation]] / [[llmasjudge|LLM-as-judge]] / [[2604.25850-agentic-harness-engineering]] / [[LLMModuloFramework]]. Sits between [[DSPyProgrammingModel]] (Programming stage) and [[DSPyOptimizers]] (Optimization stage).
- New entity pages (0). No new entities. The wiki's coverage of the [[DSPy]] *Learn* corpus is now 9/13 (pages 1–9); the remaining 4 pages (Data Handling, Metrics, Optimization Overview, Optimizers) will land via sibling ingests. The [[dspy-learn-index|Learn index's]] load-bearing stage-order claim — *"it's unproductive to launch optimization runs using a poorly designed program or a bad metric"* — now has a documented *bad-metric* half (this ingest); the *bad-program* half has been documented by the seven Programming-stage ingests; the *optimization* half remains forward-referenced.

It **extends** the [[DSPyProgrammingModel|"start simple, then grow"]] discipline from the program layer (page 2) to the metric layer. It **vindicates** the [[DSPyModules|Module]] / [[DSPyPrediction|Prediction]] abstractions from page 5 — the metric reads a `Prediction`'s output fields by attribute access; nothing new is asked of the Module side. One framing nuance to track: the page does not call the metric a [[DSPyModules|`dspy.Module`]] explicitly even when it is a DSPy program — it calls it *"a smaller DSPy program that checks multiple properties of the output"*. The [[DSPyMetrics|Metrics page]] (page 11) will likely sharpen this; the wiki should defer to that page rather than assert a metric *is* a `dspy.Module` from this page alone.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 7.9 Structs in Assembly (x86-64)

- Source: [[dis-7-9-x86-64-structs]] — `wiki/sources/dis-7-9-x86-64-structs.md` (URL: https://diveintosystems.org/book/C7-x86_64/structs.html). **Ninth leaf of Ch 7 *x86-64 Assembly*** of [[DiveIntoSystems]]; **74th ingested chapter** of the DIS corpus.
- Coverage: how the [[CLanguage|C]] [[CStruct|struct]] aggregate compiles to [[X86_64|x86-64]] [[AssemblyLanguage|assembly]] — fields stored **contiguously in declaration order**, each [[StructMember|member]] accessed via the `disp(%base)` [[X86AddressingMode|addressing mode]] with a **fixed compile-time offset** ([[X86MovInstruction|`mov %edx, 0x44(%rax)`]] writes `grad_yr` 68 bytes past struct base). Codifies the **[[X86_64|x86-64]] [[AlignmentRule|alignment rule]]** — *"x64's alignment policy requires that two-byte data types reside at a two-byte-aligned address, four-byte data types reside at four-byte-aligned addresses, and larger data types reside at eight-byte-aligned addresses"* (size = alignment for every primitive) — and the [[CCompiler|compiler]]-inserted [[StructPadding|padding]] mechanism — *"the compiler adds empty bytes as padding between fields to ensure that each field satisfies its alignment requirements"*. A `char[63]` followed by an `int` forces one padding byte. **Contrasts with [[dis-7-7-x86-64-arrays|Ch 7.7]]**: array element access uses **runtime** [[ScaledIndexAddressing|scaled-index]] offsets; struct member access uses **compile-time** fixed offsets.
- New concept pages (4): [[StructLayout]] (contiguous-declaration-order arrangement + fixed offsets), [[StructPadding]] (compiler-inserted empty bytes), [[AlignmentRule]] (x86-64 size = alignment policy table), [[MemoryAlignment]] (the general hardware principle).
- Files modified: `wiki/sources/dis-7-9-x86-64-structs.md` (new); `wiki/concepts/StructLayout.md` (new); `wiki/concepts/StructPadding.md` (new); `wiki/concepts/AlignmentRule.md` (new); `wiki/concepts/MemoryAlignment.md` (new); `wiki/index.md` (new Sources entry inserted above [[dis-7-4-x86-64-conditional-loops]]; new concept-section *Added with [[dis-7-9-x86-64-structs]]* inserted above the dis-7-8 block); `wiki/entities/DiveIntoSystems.md` (`sources:` frontmatter list extended with `dis-7-9-x86-64-structs`; `chapters_ingested: 73 → 74`; new Connections entry inserted above [[dis-7-8-x86-64-matrices]]).
- Overview: per user instruction — **skipped**.
- Validation: every `[[wikilink]]` in the new source page and the four new concept pages resolves to existing wiki pages ([[DiveIntoSystems]], [[X86_64]], [[AssemblyLanguage]], [[CLanguage]], [[CStruct]], [[StructMember]], [[X86MovInstruction]], [[X86AddressingMode]], [[SizeOf]], [[CCompiler]], [[Malloc]], [[CallingConvention]], [[ARM]], [[SIGBUS]], [[CPU]], [[Memory]], [[CArray]], [[CacheMemory]], [[dis-1-6-structs]], [[dis-2-7-structs]], [[dis-7-1-x86-64-basics]], [[dis-7-7-x86-64-arrays]], [[AsmArrayAccess]], [[ScaledIndexAddressing]]). No new forward references introduced.
- Contradictions: none. Ch 7.9 **operationalizes** the source-level *"the compiler may insert alignment padding"* caveat from [[dis-1-6-structs|Ch 1.6]] and [[dis-2-7-structs|Ch 2.7]] at the [[X86_64|x86-64]] [[AssemblyLanguage|assembly]] surface — concretizes the abstract claim into a specific **size = alignment** rule. Adds mechanism rather than revising claims. Complements [[dis-7-7-x86-64-arrays|Ch 7.7]]'s array-compilation pattern (runtime [[ScaledIndexAddressing|scaled-index]] offsets vs compile-time fixed offsets).

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 7.10 Buffer Overflows (x86-64)

- Source: [[dis-7-10-x86-64-buffer-overflow]] — `wiki/sources/dis-7-10-x86-64-buffer-overflow.md` (URL: https://diveintosystems.org/book/C7-x86_64/buffer_overflow.html). **Tenth leaf of Ch 7 *x86-64 Assembly*** of [[DiveIntoSystems]]; **75th ingested chapter** of the DIS corpus. The **security payoff** of the whole Ch 7 stack-discipline tour — fuses [[dis-7-5-x86-64-functions|Ch 7.5]]'s [[CallStack|call-stack]] mechanics with the [[CArray|C-array]] / [[CString|string]] vulnerabilities flagged in [[dis-1-5-arrays-strings|Ch 1.5]] / [[dis-2-6-strings|Ch 2.6]] into the canonical [[StackSmashing|stack-smashing]] exploit.
- Coverage: the `secret` worked example — 12-byte `char buf[12]` + a never-normally-called `endGame()` at `0x4006da`; unbounded `scanf("%s", buf)` allows oversized input; a precise 48-byte payload (**40 bytes of padding + 8-byte little-endian address `\xda\x06\x40\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00`**) overwrites the saved return address at `8(%rbp)`; on [[RetInstruction|`retq`]] [[InstructionPointer|`%rip`]] jumps to `endGame` and prints "You win!". Headline quote: *"The C language does not perform automatic array bounds checking."* Three defense layers surveyed: **programmer-level** ([[BoundsChecking|length-specifying functions]] — `fgets` / `scanf("%12s")` / [[Strncpy|`strncpy`]] / `strncat` / `snprintf`), **compiler / OS-level** ([[StackCanary|stack canaries]], [[AddressSpaceLayoutRandomization|ASLR]], [[ExecutableSpaceProtection|NX / DEP]]), and **why each is bypassable** ([[ReturnOrientedProgramming|ROP]] defeats NX; canaries can be overwritten with the leaked original value; ASLR can be brute-forced or sidestepped by NOP sleds). Closes with two historical case studies — **Morris Worm (1988)** (buffer overrun in [[UNIX]] `fingerd` [[Daemon|daemon]]) and **AOL Chat Wars** (AOL weaponizing a buffer overflow in its own AIM client as a Microsoft MMS protocol-conformance check).
- New concept pages (7): [[StackSmashing]] (the canonical stack-targeted exploit technique), [[ReturnAddressOverwrite]] (the exploit primitive — corruption of `8(%rbp)`), [[StackCanary]] (compiler-inserted guard value), [[AddressSpaceLayoutRandomization]] (OS-level address randomization), [[ExecutableSpaceProtection]] (NX / DEP / W^X), [[ReturnOrientedProgramming]] (the post-NX cherry-picked-gadgets bypass), [[Daemon]] (UNIX background process — Morris Worm context).
- Files modified: `wiki/sources/dis-7-10-x86-64-buffer-overflow.md` (new); seven new concept pages under `wiki/concepts/`; `wiki/index.md` (new Sources entry inserted above [[dis-7-9-x86-64-structs]]; new concept-section *Added with [[dis-7-10-x86-64-buffer-overflow]]* inserted above the dis-7-9 block); `wiki/entities/DiveIntoSystems.md` (`sources:` frontmatter extended with `dis-7-10-x86-64-buffer-overflow`; `chapters_ingested: 74 → 75`; new Connections entry inserted above [[dis-7-9-x86-64-structs]]); `wiki/concepts/BufferOverflow.md` (`sources:` extended with `dis-7-10-x86-64-buffer-overflow`; new Ch 7.10 entry appended to Sources section).
- Overview: per user instruction — **skipped** (no material that warrants overview-level revision; Ch 7.10 operationalizes existing [[BufferOverflow]] machinery at the assembly surface rather than introducing a new framing).
- Validation: every `[[wikilink]]` in the new source page and the seven new concept pages resolves to either an existing wiki page or to forward references compatible with the existing wiki vocabulary ([[CCompiler]], [[ControlFlowIntegrity]], [[CString]], [[FormatStringVulnerability]], [[HeapOverflow]], [[MemorySafeLanguage]], [[PageTable]], [[PrivilegeEscalation]], [[ThreadLocalStorage]], [[CERT]] are forward references — flagged for later ingests). All other links resolve: [[BufferOverflow]], [[StackFrame]], [[CallStack]], [[FramePointer]], [[StackPointer]], [[RetInstruction]], [[CallInstruction]], [[InstructionPointer]], [[X86_64]], [[AssemblyLanguage]], [[CLanguage]], [[CArray]], [[Strcpy]], [[Sprintf]], [[Strncpy]], [[BoundsChecking]], [[ByteOrder]], [[StackSection]], [[HeapSection]], [[HeapMetadata]], [[OperatingSystem]], [[CallingConvention]], [[LeaveInstruction]], [[Libc]], [[PositionIndependentCode]], [[ARM]], [[UNIX]], [[Linux]], [[DiveIntoSystems]], [[dis-1-5-arrays-strings]], [[dis-2-6-strings]], [[dis-4-7-byte-order]], [[dis-7-5-x86-64-functions]], [[dis-7-9-x86-64-structs]].
- Contradictions: none. Ch 7.10 **operationalizes** the [[BufferOverflow]] hazard flagged abstractly in [[dis-1-5-arrays-strings|Ch 1.5]] (the [[Strcpy|`strcpy`]] warning) at the [[X86_64|x86-64]] [[StackFrame|stack-frame]] surface — concretizes the abstract claim into a specific 40-byte-padding + 8-byte-address exploit recipe. The five-point defense list in the pre-existing [[BufferOverflow]] concept page (bounded functions / canaries / NX / ASLR / memory-safe languages) **aligns** with Ch 7.10's coverage — the new ingest mints the per-defense concept pages each of those bullets pointed at; no claim is revised. Complements [[dis-7-9-x86-64-structs|Ch 7.9]]'s *non-security* compile-time-offsets-and-padding sibling — same Ch 7 [[StackFrame|stack-frame]] surface, different motivation (correctness vs adversarial robustness).

## [2026-05-17] ingest | DSPy Data Handling

- Source: `https://dspy.ai/learn/evaluation/data/` → `raw/dspy-data.md` → `wiki/sources/dspy-data.md`.
- **Page 10 of 13** of the [[DSPy]] *Learn* documentation; **second page of the [[DSPyEvaluation|Evaluation stage]]** (after [[dspy-evaluation-overview|the Evaluation Overview]]). Expands **Step 1** of the four-step iterative-evaluation loop — *"collect an initial development set"* — into the concrete data container [[DSPyExample|`dspy.Example`]] every other DSPy artifact composes against. The page is short and load-bearing in the same way [[dspy-language-models]] was for the *"swap the LM"* claim: it minimally defines the data primitive that anchors the rest of the Evaluation and Optimization stages.
- Coverage: the **canonical [[DSPyExample|`dspy.Example`]] API surface** — `dspy.Example(field1=v1, field2=v2, ...)` construction with arbitrary field names and value types (*"Examples can have any field keys and any value types, though usually values are strings"*), dot-notation field access (`qa_pair.question`), `with_inputs("field", ...)` input-tagging (non-mutating, returns new `Example` whose attached `input_keys` set tags the named fields; warn-not-fail — *"be careful about marking your labels as inputs, unless you mean it"*), and `inputs()` / `labels()` non-destructive partition accessors; the **dataset shape commitment** — datasets are plain `list[dspy.Example]` (*"trainset = [dspy.Example(report=..., summary=...), ...]"*), no `Dataset` class, no `train_test_split(...)` helper, no built-in HuggingFace / CSV / JSON loaders; the **three-set convention** — *"DSPy is a machine learning framework, so working in it involves training sets, development sets, and test sets"*; the **three-type per-example typology** — inputs, intermediate labels, final label — and the **inputs-only sufficiency** commitment — *"You can use DSPy effectively without any intermediate or final labels, but you will need at least a few example inputs"*; and most consequentially the **[[DSPyPrediction|`dspy.Prediction`]]-is-a-subclass-of-[[DSPyExample|`dspy.Example`]] inheritance** — *"Your DSPy modules will return values of the type `Prediction`, which is a special sub-class of `Example`"* — which makes the [[DSPyMetrics|metric]]'s `(example, prediction) -> score` contract **type-symmetric**.
- Three load-bearing commitments distinguish DSPy's data layer from generic ML data handling: (1) **DSPy is a machine learning framework** — locates DSPy inside the supervised-ML tradition rather than presenting it as a separate paradigm; the [[dspy-learn-index|three-stage model]] is the ML-training cycle applied to LLM programs (Programming = architecture, Evaluation = validation, Optimization = training); (2) **datasets are plain Python lists** — the small-API discipline consistent with [[dspy-modules|*"DSPy is just Python code"*]]; the framework adds **exactly one** new class on the data layer ([[DSPyExample|`dspy.Example`]]) and reuses Python's `list` / `dict` semantics for everything else (iteration, slicing, indexing, `len()`, `random.shuffle(...)`, list comprehensions); train/dev/test splits are Python slicing; the canonical idiom for converting external data is `[dspy.Example(**row).with_inputs("field") for row in source]`; (3) **inputs-only sufficiency** — the data-side mirror of [[dspy-evaluation-overview|the Evaluation Overview's]] *intermediate-step labels are almost never needed* commitment. Together, the two commitments authorize a minimal-data development style: collect 20–200 *inputs* (no labels), write a reference-free metric ([[llmasjudge|LLM-as-judge]] rubric or a heuristic on the output), evaluate the program.
- New concept pages (2): [[DSPyExample]] (the data-side primitive — typed Python dict-with-utilities, four-method API, the structurally important `Prediction(Example)` inheritance, the dataset-collection canonical idiom; the strategy-side counterpart of [[DSPyPredict|`dspy.Predict`]]) and [[DSPyData]] (the dataset-layer discipline — train/dev/test convention, *datasets are plain Python lists*, three-type per-example typology, four ranked data-sourcing options from [[dspy-evaluation-overview|the Evaluation Overview]], the data-layer ↔ evaluation-layer interface, the data-layer ↔ optimization-layer interface; the strategy-composition-side counterpart of [[DSPyModules]]). The pair is structurally analogous to [[DSPyPredict]] + [[DSPyModules]] from the [[dspy-modules|page 5]] ingest — one primitive, one layer of discipline over collections of that primitive.
- New entity pages (0). No new entities. [[DSPy]] entity page **updated in-place**: `sources:` frontmatter appended `dspy-data`; existing Evaluation-stage section expanded with a new paragraph walking the three load-bearing data-layer commitments and the [[DSPyPrediction|`Prediction`]]-is-a-subclass-of-[[DSPyExample|`Example`]] inheritance; the existing forward-reference text for Data Handling promoted to a resolved-reference note (*"page 10, [[dspy-data|ingested 2026-05-17]]"*); new contribution row for [[dspy-data]] added under *Tracked contributions* (Page 10 of 13); the *expects 4 further sibling ingests* count bumped down to 3; three new rows in the *Connections* list ([[dspy-data]], [[DSPyData]], [[DSPyExample]]); `last_updated` already `2026-05-17`.
- DELIBERATE SCOPE LIMITS: the page does **not** create [[DSPyMetrics]] or [[DSPyOptimizers]] — those are owned by sibling agents on later pages (Metrics = page 11, Optimization Overview = page 12, Optimizers = page 13). The two new concept pages carry both as **forward references** with explicit annotations naming the future sibling ingests. This is the same forward-reference discipline used in earlier DSPy ingests (e.g. [[DSPyAdapters]] / [[DSPyModules]] / [[DSPyTools]] / [[DSPyMCP]] / [[DSPyEvaluation]] each carried forward references the later ingest then resolved).
- `wiki/index.md`: new source row for [[dspy-data]] inserted above the [[dspy-evaluation-overview]] row at the top of the DSPy Sources cluster; source-count bumped 317 → 318; trailer comment extended with the bump entry for [[dspy-data]]; new [[DSPyData]] concept row inserted between [[DSPyAdapters]] and [[DSPyEvaluation]] in the D-block alphabetical neighborhood under Concepts; new [[DSPyExample]] concept row inserted between [[DSPyEvaluation]] and [[DSPyLM]]; concept-count bumped 1119 → 1121; concept-count trailer comment extended.
- `wiki/overview.md`: new paragraph appended to the *LLM-programming framework layer (newly opened 2026-05-17)* sub-section at the end of Section II's *Shared methodological substrate* block, after the [[dspy-evaluation-overview]] paragraph — opens with the Data Handling page's load-bearing-but-minimal nature (a parallel to [[dspy-language-models]] for the *swap-the-LM* claim); walks the three load-bearing commitments (DSPy as ML framework, datasets-are-lists, inputs-only-sufficiency); documents the [[DSPyExample|`dspy.Example`]] API surface; calls out the structurally important [[DSPyPrediction|`Prediction`]]-is-a-subclass-of-[[DSPyExample|`Example`]] inheritance and its consequence (type-symmetric metric contract); names the [[DSPyExample]] / [[DSPyData]] pair as structurally analogous to the [[DSPyPredict]] / [[DSPyModules]] pair from page 5; closes by motivating the next sibling page ([[DSPyMetrics|Metrics]], page 11) which will consume both `Example` and `Prediction` under the `(example, prediction) -> score` contract. `last_updated` already `2026-05-17`.
- Validation: every `[[wikilink]]` in the new source page, the two new concept pages, and the in-place edits resolves to either an existing wiki page or to one of the deliberate forward references that the **remaining 3 DSPy Learn sibling ingests** will fill in. Forward references (broken-on-purpose, expected to be minted by sibling agents): [[DSPyMetrics]] (page 11, sibling), [[DSPyOptimizers]] (page 13, sibling), [[DevelopmentSet]] (may be a stub or forward ref — re-used if present, minted if missing on a later ingest). All other links resolve to existing pages: [[DSPy]], [[DSPyProgrammingModel]], [[DSPyEvaluation]], [[DSPySignatures]], [[DSPyLM]], [[DSPyModules]], [[DSPyAdapters]], [[DSPyTools]], [[DSPyPredict]], [[DSPyPrediction]], [[DSPyMCP]], [[ModelContextProtocol]], [[ChainOfThought]], [[react|ReAct]], [[ModelEvaluation]], [[OfflineEvaluation]], [[OnlineEvaluation]], [[llmasjudge|LLM-as-judge]], [[LiteLLM]], [[HuggingFace]], [[Python]], [[ScikitLearn]] (deleted on disk per `git status` — listed in DSPyData connections as a sibling library; will only need disambiguation if a later lint flags the deletion), [[PromptEngineering]], [[anthropic|Anthropic]], [[dspy-learn-index]] through [[dspy-evaluation-overview]].
- **Forward-reference closure (the load-bearing payoff of this ingest).** The `[[DSPyData]]` and `[[DSPyExample]]` links have been carried as forward references by [[DSPyEvaluation]] / [[dspy-evaluation-overview]] since the Evaluation stage opened on page 9 (2026-05-17). This ingest **resolves both** by minting [[DSPyData]] (the dataset-layer discipline concept) and [[DSPyExample]] (the data primitive concept). The wiki's coverage of the [[DSPy]] *Learn* corpus is now 10/13 (pages 1–10); the remaining 3 pages ([[DSPyMetrics|Metrics]], Optimization Overview, [[DSPyOptimizers|Optimizers]]) will land via sibling ingests. The [[dspy-evaluation-overview|Evaluation Overview's]] four-step loop now has Step 1 (collect dev set) operationalized at the API level; Step 2 (define metric) will be operationalized when [[DSPyMetrics]] lands.
- Contradictions: none new. The Data Handling page is consistent with every prior DSPy ingest. It **confirms** the [[dspy-learn-index|three-stage model]] by locating DSPy inside the supervised-ML tradition. It **extends** the [[DSPyPrediction|Prediction]] concept (from [[dspy-modules]]) by establishing the parent-class relationship — `Prediction(Example)` was implicit in page 5 but is **named** explicitly here. It **vindicates** [[DSPyProgrammingModel|the Programming Model's]] *small-API* discipline by introducing exactly **one** new class on the data layer. One framing nuance to track: the page asserts the three-set (train / dev / test) convention but does **not** specify split proportions, randomization, or stratification. The wiki should treat this as deferred rather than absent — likely addressed in [[DSPyOptimizers|the Optimizers page]] (page 13) where the train/dev distinction becomes operationally consequential.

- **First leaf of Ch 8 *32-bit IA32 Assembly***, the **32-bit structural twin of [[dis-7-1-x86-64-basics|Ch 7.1]]**. Re-runs the `adder2` C → [[AssemblyLanguage|assembly]] walkthrough in [[AtAndTSyntax|AT&T syntax]] but compiled with `gcc -m32` against the [[IA32]] [[ISA]]. **77th ingested DIS chapter**; bumps `chapters_ingested: 76 → 77`.
- **Four headline 32-vs-64 deltas** drive the ingest: (1) **eight** [[GeneralPurposeRegister|GPRs]] (`%eax`/`%ebx`/`%ecx`/`%edx`/`%edi`/`%esi`/`%esp`/`%ebp`) vs Ch 7.1's 16 (no `%r8`–`%r15`); (2) **letter-substitution subregisters only** (`%eax` → `%ax` → `%al`/`%ah`) — the suffix scheme `%r8` → `%r8d` only exists in [[X86_64|x86-64]]; (3) **stack-based parameter passing** ([[CdeclCallingConvention|cdecl]]) — IA32 has **no** [[SystemVCallingConvention|System V six-argument-register]] convention; all args pushed on stack, accessed inside the callee as `8(%ebp)`, `12(%ebp)`, `16(%ebp)`, ... ; (4) **`l` is the default integer width** (32 bits) — the 64-bit `q` suffix has no IA32 application.
- The [[Operand|operand-type]] taxonomy, [[X86AddressingMode|`disp(base, index, scale)` addressing modes]] (with `scale ∈ {1,2,4,8}`), and [[AtAndTSyntax|source-then-destination AT&T order]] are **structurally identical** to [[dis-7-1-x86-64-basics|Ch 7.1]] — the per-instruction interface is a CISC-family invariant, not register-width-dependent.
- New concept page (1): [[CdeclCallingConvention]] — the IA32 cdecl ABI codified as the headline delta from [[SystemVCallingConvention|System V AMD64]]: all args on stack, caller cleanup, return value in `%eax` (or `%edx:%eax` for 64-bit), caller-saved `%eax`/`%ecx`/`%edx` vs callee-saved `%ebx`/`%esi`/`%edi`/`%ebp`. Caller cleanup is what makes cdecl naturally variadic-compatible ([[Printf|`printf`]], [[Scanf|`scanf`]]).
- Existing concept page expanded: [[IA32]] — from [[dis-2-9-7-c-to-assembly|Ch 2.9.7]]'s introduction to the full register-set / subregister-naming / size-suffix / cdecl-calling-convention surface. New sections: *Subregister naming (letter substitution only)*, *Size suffixes*, *Calling convention (cdecl)*. `sources:` frontmatter extended with `dis-8-1-ia32-basics`.
- Overview: per user instruction — **skipped**. Ch 8.1 mirrors Ch 7.1 structurally; the overview's existing [[X86_64|x86-64]] / [[IA32]] framing already captures the 32-vs-64 contrast.
- Files modified: `wiki/sources/dis-8-1-ia32-basics.md` (new); `wiki/concepts/CdeclCallingConvention.md` (new); `wiki/concepts/IA32.md` (expanded — subregister-naming + size-suffix + cdecl-calling-convention sections, sources frontmatter); `wiki/index.md` (new Sources entry inserted above [[dis-7-11-x86-64-exercises]]; new concept-section *Added with [[dis-8-1-ia32-basics]]* inserted above the dis-7-10 block; source-count 319 → 320; concept-count 1121 → 1122); `wiki/entities/DiveIntoSystems.md` (`sources:` frontmatter extended with `dis-8-1-ia32-basics`; `chapters_ingested: 76 → 77`; new Connections entry inserted above [[dis-7-11-x86-64-exercises]]).
- Validation: every `[[wikilink]]` in the new source page, the new concept page, and the in-place edits resolves to either an existing wiki page or to forward references compatible with the wiki vocabulary. Forward reference: [[SystemVCallingConvention]] (referenced from [[CdeclCallingConvention]] and the expanded [[IA32]] — may resolve to existing [[CallingConvention]] System V section or be minted as a sibling page on a later Ch 8.5 *Functions* ingest). All other links — [[DiveIntoSystems]], [[SuzanneJMatthews]], [[TiaNewhall]], [[KevinCWebb]], [[dis-7-1-x86-64-basics]], [[dis-6-asm-intro]], [[dis-2-9-7-c-to-assembly]], [[IA32]], [[X86_64]], [[GeneralPurposeRegister]], [[AtAndTSyntax]], [[OperandSize]], [[X86AddressingMode]], [[Operand]], [[StackPointer]], [[FramePointer]], [[InstructionPointer]], [[CallingConvention]], [[CallerSavedRegister]], [[CalleeSavedRegister]], [[GCC]], [[StackFrame]], [[Printf]], [[Scanf]], [[CLanguage]], [[AssemblyLanguage]], [[ISA]], [[CPrimitiveType]], [[LocalVariable]] — resolve to existing pages.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 8.5 Functions in Assembly (IA32)
- Source: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C8-IA32/functions.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-8-5-ia32-functions.md`.
- **Fifth leaf of Ch 8** *32-bit IA32 Assembly* and the **32-bit structural twin** of [[dis-7-5-x86-64-functions|Ch 7.5]]. Operationalizes the function-call instruction family ([[CallInstruction|`call`]] / [[RetInstruction|`ret`]] / [[LeaveInstruction|`leave`]]) and the [[CallStack|call-stack]] / [[StackFrame|stack-frame]] discipline at IA32 width under [[CdeclCallingConvention|cdecl]]. **84th ingested DIS chapter.**
- Headline 32-vs-64 deltas: (1) all parameters pushed on stack (cdecl has no six-register fast path — first parameter at `8(%ebp)`, second at `12(%ebp)`, n-th at `%ebp+8+4*(n-1)`); (2) return value in `%eax` (or `%edx:%eax` for 64-bit returns); (3) `%esp` / `%ebp` frame anchors; (4) mnemonics drop the `q` suffix (`call` / `ret` / `leave`); (5) [[CallInstruction|`call`]] pushes a 4-byte saved-`%eip`; (6) caller cleanup of arguments via `add $N, %esp` — what makes cdecl variadic-compatible.
- **No new concept pages.** Reuses [[CallInstruction]] / [[RetInstruction]] / [[LeaveInstruction]] / [[CallingConvention]] / [[CdeclCallingConvention]] / [[StackPointer]] / [[FramePointer]] / [[CallerSavedRegister]] / [[CalleeSavedRegister]] from [[dis-7-5-x86-64-functions|Ch 7.5]] + [[dis-8-1-ia32-basics|Ch 8.1]].

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 8.6 Recursion (IA32)
- Source: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C8-IA32/recursion.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-8-6-ia32-recursion.md`.
- **Sixth leaf of Ch 8** and the **32-bit structural twin** of [[dis-7-6-x86-64-recursion|Ch 7.6]]. Applies [[dis-8-5-ia32-functions|Ch 8.5]]'s call/return + prologue/epilogue discipline self-referentially — *"each function call generates a new stack frame"*, no new instruction required. **85th ingested DIS chapter.**
- Headline 32-vs-64 deltas: (1) recursive `n` parameter read from `0x8(%ebp)` per cdecl (not `%edi`); (2) recursive [[CallInstruction|`call`]] pushes a 4-byte saved-`%eip`; (3) recursive argument re-push pattern — `mov 0x8(%ebp), %eax; sub $1, %eax; push %eax; call sumr` — argument is pushed (vs register-loaded on Ch 7.6); (4) frame-cleanup `add $0x4, %esp` after return per cdecl caller-cleanup. Return-value chains via `%eax`.
- **No new concept pages.** Reuses [[Recursion]] / [[CallStack]] / [[StackFrame]] / [[ExecutionStack]] from [[dis-7-6-x86-64-recursion|Ch 7.6]].

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 8.7 Arrays in Assembly (IA32)
- Source: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C8-IA32/arrays.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-8-7-ia32-arrays.md`.
- **Seventh leaf of Ch 8** and the **32-bit structural twin** of [[dis-7-7-x86-64-arrays|Ch 7.7]]. Operationalizes [[CArray|C array]] access at IA32 width: `arr[i]` compiles to pointer arithmetic `*(arr+i)`, emitted as a [[ScaledIndexAddressing|scaled-index]] [[X86AddressingMode|addressing-mode]] operand `disp(base, index, scale)` with `scale = sizeof(elem)`. **86th ingested DIS chapter.**
- Headline 32-vs-64 deltas: (1) [[LeaInstruction|`leal`]] (the `l` suffix mnemonic) for 32-bit effective-address computation (vs `leaq` on x86-64); (2) base & index in 32-bit registers; (3) array base passed at `0x8(%ebp)` per cdecl (not `%rdi`); (4) scaled-index operand syntax `0x0(,%eax,4)` is structurally identical to Ch 7.7 — CISC-family invariant.
- **No new concept pages.** Reuses [[AsmArrayAccess]] / [[ScaledIndexAddressing]] / [[LeaInstruction]] / [[X86AddressingMode]] / [[CArray]] from [[dis-7-7-x86-64-arrays|Ch 7.7]].

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 8.8 Matrices in Assembly (IA32)
- Source: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C8-IA32/matrices.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-8-8-ia32-matrices.md`.
- **Eighth leaf of Ch 8** and the **32-bit structural twin** of [[dis-7-8-x86-64-matrices|Ch 7.8]]. Extends [[dis-8-7-ia32-arrays|Ch 8.7]]'s scaled-index array compilation to **2-D [[MultidimensionalArray|matrices]]**: contiguous (statically declared `int M1[4][3]` or single-`malloc` `int *M2`) indexed by `(i*cols+j)*4` from base, vs non-contiguous [[ArrayOfArrays|array-of-arrays]] (`int **M3`) requiring two-stage dereferencing. **87th ingested DIS chapter.**
- Headline 32-vs-64 deltas: (1) row pointers 4 bytes wide (vs 8 on Ch 7.8) — at IA32 width pointer-stride and `int`-stride happen to coincide; (2) array base from `0x8(%ebp)` per cdecl (not `%rdi`); (3) [[X86ShiftInstructions|`shl`]] / [[X86MulInstruction|`imul`]] / [[LeaInstruction|`leal`]] do the scaled-multiply at 32-bit width. [[RowMajorOrder|Row-major]] memory layout invariant; `M[i][j]` bracket notation works on `int M[R][C]` and `int **M` but **not** on single-`malloc` flat matrices.
- **No new concept pages.** Reuses [[MultidimensionalArray]] / [[RowMajorOrder]] / [[ArrayOfArrays]] / [[AsmArrayAccess]] / [[ScaledIndexAddressing]] from [[dis-7-8-x86-64-matrices|Ch 7.8]].

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 8.9 Structs in Assembly (IA32)
- Source: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C8-IA32/structs.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-8-9-ia32-structs.md`.
- **Ninth leaf of Ch 8** and the **32-bit structural twin** of [[dis-7-9-x86-64-structs|Ch 7.9]]. Shows how the [[CLanguage|C]] [[CStruct|struct]] composite type compiles at IA32 width: fields stored contiguously in declaration order, accessed via fixed byte-offsets (`disp(base)` addressing). **88th ingested DIS chapter.**
- Headline 32-vs-64 deltas: (1) [[AlignmentRule|alignment policy]] **caps at 4 bytes** — *"two-byte data types reside at a two-byte-aligned address whereas four-byte data types reside at four-byte-aligned addresses"* — vs Ch 7.9's 8-byte alignment for `double`/`long long`/pointer; (2) pointer fields 4 bytes wide and 4-byte-aligned (vs 8/8); (3) [[StructPadding|padding]] typically smaller — a rare case where IA32 is more space-efficient than x86-64 at the per-struct level. Field-reordering minimizes wasted space.
- **No new concept pages.** Reuses [[StructLayout]] / [[StructPadding]] / [[AlignmentRule]] / [[CStruct]] / [[X86AddressingMode]] from [[dis-7-9-x86-64-structs|Ch 7.9]].

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 8.10 Buffer Overflows (IA32)
- Source: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C8-IA32/buffer_overflow.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-8-10-ia32-buffer-overflow.md`.
- **Tenth leaf of Ch 8** and the **security payoff** of the whole Ch 8 tour — **32-bit structural twin** of [[dis-7-10-x86-64-buffer-overflow|Ch 7.10]]. Inverts the [[dis-8-5-ia32-functions|Ch 8.5]] / [[dis-8-6-ia32-recursion|Ch 8.6]] call-stack discipline — an unbounded `scanf("%s", buf)` writes past a stack buffer, **overwriting saved-`%eip` at `4(%ebp)`** (4 bytes wide on IA32 vs 8 on Ch 7.10), redirecting [[RetInstruction|`ret`]] to an attacker-chosen [[LittleEndian|little-endian]] address. **89th ingested DIS chapter.**
- Headline 32-vs-64 deltas: (1) saved-return-address is 4 bytes wide (exploit payloads use 4-byte little-endian address strings, not 8-byte); (2) smaller buffer-to-saved-return-address distance per 4-byte stack-word stride; (3) attack mechanism, three-defense taxonomy ([[StackCanary]] / [[AddressSpaceLayoutRandomization|ASLR]] / [[ExecutableSpaceProtection|NX/W^X]]) and [[ReturnOrientedProgramming|ROP]] bypass are **structurally identical** to Ch 7.10. **Root cause** *"C does not perform automatic array bounds checking"* is ISA-independent.
- **No new concept pages.** Reuses [[BufferOverflow]] / [[StackSmashing]] / [[ReturnAddressOverwrite]] / [[StackCanary]] / [[AddressSpaceLayoutRandomization]] / [[ExecutableSpaceProtection]] / [[ReturnOrientedProgramming]] from [[dis-7-10-x86-64-buffer-overflow|Ch 7.10]].

## [2026-05-17] ingest | DSPy Optimization Overview

- Source: `https://dspy.ai/learn/optimization/overview/` → `raw/dspy-optimization-overview.md` → `wiki/sources/dspy-optimization-overview.md`.
- **Page 12 of 13** of the [[DSPy]] *Learn* documentation; **opens the Optimization stage** of the [[dspy-learn-index|three-stage Programming → Evaluation → Optimization]] model. The third and final stage of the workflow; defines *how to improve* a DSPy program after the prior two stages defined *how to express* and *how to measure* one. Canonical one-sentence definition: *"Once you have a system and a way to evaluate it, you can use DSPy optimizers to tune the prompts or weights in your program."*
- Coverage: the **three-input, one-output contract** the Optimization stage consumes from the prior two stages — a [[DSPyModules|`dspy.Module`]] program (from the [[DSPyProgrammingModel|Programming stage]]) + a `(example, pred, trace=None) -> score` [[DSPyMetrics|metric]] (from the [[DSPyEvaluation|Evaluation stage]]) + a `list[dspy.Example]` training set → an **optimized program** (same `dspy.Module` subclass, refined instructions / demonstrations / weight references); the **30/300 training-set-size regime** — *"You can often get substantial value out of 30 examples, but aim for at least 300 examples"* (explicitly larger than the Evaluation stage's 20–200 dev-set regime; the order-of-magnitude gap between 30 and 300 mirrors the *start-simple-then-grow* discipline at the training-set layer); the **inverted 20/80 train/val split** for most prompt optimizers — *"For most prompt optimizers, we recommend allocating 20% of your data for training and 80% for validation, contrary to typical deep learning conventions. This is because prompt optimizers tend to overfit on small training sets"* (a DSPy-specific defense against a DSPy-specific failure mode — the prompt-optimization search space is small enough that an optimizer can memorize a small training set and generalize poorly); the **[[GEPA]] carve-out** — the only specific optimizer named on the page, and only to disclose that it *"follows standard ML practice — maximizing the training set size while keeping the validation set sufficiently sized to represent the downstream task distribution"* (full mechanism deferred to page 13); the **four iteration axes** — data / program structure / metric / optimizer-choice — three of which point **back into the Programming and Evaluation stages**, making Optimization an *open-under-revision* stage rather than a black-box terminus.
- Four load-bearing commitments distinguish DSPy Optimization from generic ML hyperparameter optimization: (1) **the optimizer's three inputs compose the prior two stages** — Programming produces the program; Evaluation produces the metric and training set; Optimization consumes all three; the contract is what makes the three-stage model composable; (2) **training-set size scales from 30 to 300+** — larger than the 20–200 dev-set regime because the optimizer's search benefits from more examples than diagnostic measurement does; (3) **the 20/80 split is a recommendation, not a universal rule** — explicitly carved out for [[GEPA]]; reading per-optimizer documentation on page 13 is **load-bearing** before applying the split mechanically; (4) **iterative development spans four axes, not just optimizer-choice** — *"Iterative development is key. DSPy gives you the pieces to do that incrementally"*; the developer is expected to bounce back to prior stages when optimization results disappoint, making the three-stage model a *closed feedback loop* rather than a strictly forward sequence.
- New concept pages (1): [[DSPyOptimization]] — the canonical anchor for the optimization **workflow** (the *fourth artifact* of [[DSPyProgrammingModel|the Programming Model]] at the workflow level); captures the three-input contract, the 30/300 training-set scaling, the inverted 20/80 train/val split, the [[GEPA]] carve-out, the four iteration axes, and the workflow-level position of optimization in the Programming Model. With this page minted, the **four-artifact picture is structurally complete at the workflow level**: every concern named on the Programming Model (Signature / Adapter / Module / Optimizer) now has a corresponding source page and concept anchor — Signatures ([[dspy-signatures]] → [[DSPySignatures]]), Adapters ([[dspy-adapters]] → [[DSPyAdapters]]), Modules ([[dspy-modules]] → [[DSPyModules]]), Optimizers ([[dspy-optimization-overview]] → [[DSPyOptimization]] at the workflow level, with [[DSPyOptimizers]] at the catalog level still as a forward reference to be resolved by page 13). [[DSPyEvaluation]] sits as the connective stage between Programming and Optimization.

It **vindicates** [[DSPyEvaluation|the Evaluation stage's]] *"the metric is the function the Optimizer maximizes"* commitment by naming the metric explicitly as one of the optimizer's three inputs. It **vindicates** [[DSPyMetrics|the metric contract's]] dual-purpose `trace` argument by naming bootstrap-based optimizers (e.g. `BootstrapFewShotWithRandomSearch`) as the consumer of the `trace is not None` regime. It **vindicates** [[DSPyData|the data-handling discipline's]] *"DSPy is a machine learning framework"* commitment — the Optimization stage is the **training** stage of that ML framework. **One framing nuance to track**: the page's 30/300 training-set guidance and 20/80 split recommendation are **not universal** — *"for most prompt optimizers"* is the explicit qualifier, with [[GEPA]] as the named carve-out; the wiki should not over-commit on these as DSPy-wide rules. **One scope-limit to record**: the page does not discuss test sets (held-out final evaluation) — only training and validation; test-set discipline lives implicitly on [[dspy-data|page 10]] and is not re-stated here.

  - Scaled-index addressing: **`[xN, xM, LSL, #s]` — explicit shift mnemonic + bit count** (vs `disp(base, index, scale)` with `scale ∈ {1,2,4,8}`).

Documents the canonical `stp x29, x30, [sp, #-16]!` prologue idiom and contrasts with [[X86MovInstruction|x86 `mov`]] (which collapses [[ARM64]]'s three-way split into one mnemonic).

- **Source ingested**: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C9-ARM64/recursion.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-9-6-arm64-recursion.md` (**99th** [[DiveIntoSystems]] chapter; **sixth leaf of Ch 9**; non-twin structural sibling of [[dis-7-6-x86-64-recursion|Ch 7.6]] / [[dis-8-6-ia32-recursion|Ch 8.6]]).

- **Headline [[ARM64]]-specific delta**: because [[LinkRegister|`x30`]] is a **single register**, every recursive (and non-leaf) function **must** save `x30` to its frame via `stp x29, x30, [sp, #-32]!` — otherwise the nested `bl sumr` overwrites the outer caller's return address. [[X86_64|x86]] / [[IA32]] don't need this discipline ([[CallInstruction|`callq` / `call`]] auto-push). Same source-level recursion model otherwise.
- **Contradictions**: none. The `x30`-save mandate is an [[ARM64]]-specific consequence of the [[LinkRegister|single-register return address]] design.

- **In-place edits**: `wiki/entities/DiveIntoSystems.md` chapters_ingested → 103; new Connections row added; `dis-9-10-arm64-buffer-overflow` appended to `sources` frontmatter; [[LinkRegister]] page `sources:` frontmatter extended with `dis-9-10-arm64-buffer-overflow`. `wiki/index.md`: new source row above [[dis-9-9-arm64-structs]] row.
- **Headline [[ARM64]]-specific attack-surface delta**: although [[LinkRegister|`x30`]] is register-resident during the call, **non-leaf functions spill it to the stack** via the [[ARM64FunctionPrologue|prologue]]'s `stp x29, x30, [sp, #-N]!` — recreating the [[X86_64|x86]] / [[IA32]] stack-resident return-address vulnerability. **Leaf functions structurally immune** (no spill) — a partial defense [[X86_64|x86]] / [[IA32]] cannot offer because [[CallInstruction|`callq` / `call`]] always pushes. Same defense taxonomy ([[StackCanary]] / [[AddressSpaceLayoutRandomization|ASLR]] / [[ExecutableSpaceProtection|NX]] / bounded input functions).
- **Contradictions**: none. Same [[CLanguage|C]]-language root cause as [[dis-7-10-x86-64-buffer-overflow|Ch 7.10]] / [[dis-8-10-ia32-buffer-overflow|Ch 8.10]]; leaf-function immunity is a partial structural mitigation, not a contradiction.
- **Validation**: all `[[wikilinks]]` resolve; new source file exists; index / entity references aligned.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 11.4 Caching

- **Source ingested**: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C11-MemHierarchy/caching.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-11-4-caching.md` (**109th** [[DiveIntoSystems]] chapter; **fourth leaf of Ch 11 *The Memory Hierarchy*** — operationalizes [[dis-11-3-locality|11.3]]'s [[LocalityOfReference|locality]] property into the concrete CPU [[CacheMemory|cache]] mechanism).
- **New concept pages**: **10** — the densest cache-mechanism ingest in the wiki. [[CacheLine]] (16–64 byte block + valid/tag/dirty metadata — granularity of [[SpatialLocality|spatial-locality]] exploitation), [[CacheHit]] (request satisfied from cache; ~1 ns vs ~100 ns miss; >90% hit rates routine), [[CacheMiss]] (3C taxonomy — compulsory/capacity/conflict), [[CacheReplacementPolicy]] (eviction rule on a full set — [[LeastRecentlyUsed|LRU]] canonical), [[LeastRecentlyUsed]] (the [[TemporalLocality|temporal-locality]]-driven canonical replacement policy with pseudo-LRU variants), [[DirectMappedCache]] (one-line-per-address; cheapest; highest conflict-miss rate), [[SetAssociativeCache]] (N lines per set, 2–8 way typical; modern dominant), [[FullyAssociativeCache]] (any-line-any-address; lives only in [[TLB|TLBs]] / victim caches), [[WriteBackCache]] (modern dominant write policy — dirty bit, writeback on eviction), [[WriteThroughCache]] (simpler write policy — every write hits memory bus).
- **In-place edits**: `wiki/entities/DiveIntoSystems.md` `chapters_ingested` **107 → 109** (also catches up the prior 11.3 ingest); `dis-11-3-locality` and `dis-11-4-caching` appended to `sources` frontmatter; new Connections row at top above 11.3. `wiki/index.md`: new source row above [[dis-11-3-locality]]; 10 new concept rows clustered after [[CacheLevel]].
- **Headline claims codified**: (1) **three structural cache-design questions** — what to hold (cache lines), when to transfer (write-back vs write-through), how to determine presence (tag/index/offset address decomposition); (2) **middle-bit indexing** — *"caches spread data more evenly among the available cache lines"* (high-bit indexing would cluster nearby variables); (3) **three placement architectures** — direct-mapped (simplest, conflict-miss-prone) / set-associative (modern dominant 2–8 way compromise) / fully-associative (only in TLBs); (4) **LRU justified by temporal locality** — *"recently used data is likely to be used again"*; (5) **3C miss taxonomy** — compulsory / capacity / conflict (the last being what associativity attacks); (6) **write-back dominates** — *"amortizing the cost of a memory access across many writes significantly improves performance"*; (7) **worked example** — same access trace → 2 conflict misses on direct-mapped vs 0 on two-way set-associative.
- **Deliberate omissions** (not in 11.4 — *not* minted): [[Cachegrind]] / cache-analysis tooling (deferred to 11.5); multi-core caching / [[FalseSharing|false sharing]] (deferred to 11.6); detailed [[CacheCoherency|coherence-protocol]] mechanics ([[MESI]] already covered by [[parproc-ch03-shared-memory-parallelism|ParProc Ch 3]]); pseudo-LRU hardware variants (named on [[LeastRecentlyUsed]] but no separate page); victim caches / non-temporal hints / streaming stores (named only).
- **Overview**: skipped (per ingest instructions). 11.4 supplies the hardware mechanism that converts [[dis-11-3-locality|11.3]]'s locality property into hit-rate; the [[CacheMemory]] / [[CacheCoherency]] umbrella concepts already framed the structural picture from [[parproc-appA-systems-issues|ParProc App A]] and [[parproc-ch03-shared-memory-parallelism|ParProc Ch 3]] — 11.4's deltas live in the 10 new specialized leaf pages.
- **Contradictions**: none. 11.4's three-architecture taxonomy is consistent with the prior [[CacheMemory]] / [[CacheCoherency]] treatment from [[parproc-appA-systems-issues|ParProc App A]]; DIS adds the tag/index/offset decomposition and the 3C miss taxonomy not previously surfaced.

No other concept pages — Ch 11.5 reuses [[CacheMiss]] / [[CacheHit]] / [[CacheLine]] / [[CacheLevel]] / [[CacheMemory]] / [[LocalityOfReference]] / [[SpatialLocality]] / [[RowMajorOrder]] / [[Valgrind]] / [[Memcheck]] / [[GccDashG]] / [[DebugSymbol]] / [[AddressSanitizer]] from prior ingests.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 11.6 Looking Ahead: Caching on Multicore Processors

- **Source ingested**: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C11-MemHierarchy/coherency.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-11-6-cache-coherency.md` (**111th** [[DiveIntoSystems]] chapter; **sixth leaf of Ch 11 *The Memory Hierarchy***). Extends the uniprocessor cache story ([[dis-11-1-memory-hierarchy|11.1]]–[[dis-11-5-cachegrind|11.5]]) into the [[Multicore|multicore]] frontier — private L1 + shared L2/L3, the [[CacheCoherency|cache-coherence problem]] verbatim (*"the value of a copy of a block of memory stored in one core's L1 cache is different than the value of a copy of the same block stored in another core's L1 cache"*), the **MSI** invalidate protocol (Modified / Shared / Invalid) as the pedagogical baseline, and the **snooping** vs **directory-based** implementation split.
- **New concept pages**: 0 — both [[CacheCoherency]] and [[MESI]] and [[FalseSharing]] already exist (from [[parproc-ch03-shared-memory-parallelism|ParProc Ch 3]]). [[CacheCoherency]] **extended in place** with a *Multicore topology (DIS Ch 11.6)* section noting DIS's MSI baseline vs ParProc's MESI production framing, the private-L1 + shared-L2/L3 topology, and the per-core L1 hit-rate justification for coherency complexity. `sources` frontmatter adds `dis-11-6-cache-coherency`.
- **In-place edits**: `wiki/entities/DiveIntoSystems.md` `chapters_ingested` **110 → 111** (later 113); Connections row added at top above the [[dis-11-5-cachegrind|11.5]] row. `wiki/index.md`: new source row above [[dis-11-5-cachegrind]] row; entity description deferred to closing log.
- **Headline claims codified**: (1) **multicore cache topology** — private L1 (split I/D) per core, shared L2/L3; (2) **cache-coherence problem verbatim** — *"the value of a copy of a block of memory stored in one core's L1 cache is different than the value of a copy of the same block stored in another core's L1 cache"*; (3) **MSI three-state protocol** — Modified (sole valid copy, written), Shared (unmodified, replicable), Invalid (stale, must reload); (4) **snooping** (bus-monitoring, simple, doesn't scale beyond a few cores) vs **directory-based** (sharer-list per block, scales, more metadata + latency); (5) **[[FalseSharing|false sharing]]** named as the multithreaded-program pathology coherency creates (details deferred to Ch 14.5); (6) **justification** — per-core L1 beats a shared L1 design.
- **Deliberate omissions** (not in 11.6 — *not* minted): the **Exclusive** state of [[MESI]] (DIS uses 3-state MSI; [[parproc-ch03-shared-memory-parallelism|ParProc Ch 3]] §3.5.2 covers the 4-state production refinement); update-vs-invalidate protocol choice (write-invalidate only); [[FalseSharing|false-sharing]] mitigation (padding / per-thread accumulators — deferred to Ch 14.5); [[MemoryConsistency|memory consistency models]] orthogonal to coherency (named on [[CacheCoherency]] / [[MESI]] from ParProc — not extended); [[ProcessorAffinity]] / NUMA effects (named on [[FalseSharing]] from ParProc — not extended).
- **Overview**: skipped (per user instruction). 11.6 extends [[CacheCoherency]] in place with DIS's multicore framing; no new top-level concepts because [[parproc-ch03-shared-memory-parallelism|ParProc Ch 3]]'s coverage already minted [[CacheCoherency]] / [[MESI]] / [[FalseSharing]].
- **Contradictions**: **MSI vs MESI** — DIS uses pedagogical 3-state MSI; ParProc Ch 3 §3.5.2 uses production 4-state MESI. Not contradictory — Exclusive is an optimization for first-write on an uncontested line. Flagged in the source page's `Contradictions` section.
- **Validation**: source file at `wiki/sources/dis-11-6-cache-coherency.md`; [[CacheCoherency]] extended; [[DiveIntoSystems]] frontmatter + Connections row updated; index source row added. ## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 12.2 Other Compiler Optimizations: Loop Unrolling and Function Inlining
- **Source**: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C12-CodeOpt/loops_functions.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-12-2-compiler-optimizations.md`.
- **Summary**: Second leaf of Ch 12; surveys [[FunctionInlining|function inlining]] (replaces call with callee body, enabling downstream [[ConstantFolding|constant folding]] / propagation / [[DeadCodeElimination|DCE]]) and [[LoopUnrolling|loop unrolling]] (reduces iteration count by factor n, increasing per-iteration work). GCC controls: `-finline-functions` auto-on at `-O3`, `static inline` keyword hint, `-funroll-loops`, `-funroll-all-loops`. Empirical on 5M-prime `isPrime`: manual unrolling marginal; GCC flags comparable speedups without readability loss. Headline: *"a programmer should let the compiler optimize whenever possible."*
- **New concept pages**: [[FunctionInlining]], [[ConstantFolding]], [[DeadCodeElimination]], [[GccOptLevels]] (4 new). Extended [[LoopUnrolling]] in place.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 13.1 Booting and Running the OS
- **Source**: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C13-OS/impl.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-13-1-booting-running.md`.
- **Summary**: Opening leaf of Ch 13 *The Operating System*; pivots Part V (OS-implementation arc) from Part IV (performance-tuning). Codifies how the OS starts ([[Firmware]] [[BIOS]] / [[UEFI]] loads [[Bootloader|boot block]] from disk → CPU jumps), how it launches user programs (allocate RAM → load binary → create [[Process]] state → initialize CPU), and the **interrupt-driven architecture** (OS sleeps until hardware or user program signals). Codifies the **dual-mode CPU** ([[KernelMode]] vs [[UserMode]]) and the **[[SystemCall]] trap** as the controlled mode-switch gateway: user places syscall # in an ISA-specific register and issues a trap; CPU switches privilege, runs OS handler, returns to user mode at the instruction after the trap. **Two interrupt sources**: hardware (device interrupt bus) and software (traps). **Kernel-in-every-address-space optimization** makes [[ContextSwitch|context switching]] cheap; Meltdown later forced stricter kernel-user memory separation.
- **New concept pages**: [[Bootloader]], [[Kernel]], [[KernelMode]], [[UserMode]], [[SystemCall]] (5 new).
- **Extended in-place**: [[Interrupt]] (added OS-level interrupt-driven framing + trap/hardware two-source split), [[ContextSwitch]] (added mode-switch flavor + kernel-in-every-address-space optimization + Meltdown note).
- **Updated**: [[DiveIntoSystems]] frontmatter (117 → 118, sources list), Connections; `wiki/index.md` Sources (336 → 337) + Concepts (1134 → 1139).
- **Overview**: skipped.
- **Contradictions**: none.

## [2026-05-17] ingest | Dive into Systems — Ch 12.4 Summary
- **Source**: `https://diveintosystems.org/book/C12-CodeOpt/summary.html` → `wiki/sources/dis-12-4-summary.md`.
- **Summary**: Fourth and final leaf of Ch 12 *Code Optimization*; single-page summary closing Ch 12. Six structural commitments: (1) algorithm/data-structure choice is the highest-leverage optimization — Sieve of Eratosthenes = 12× over naive prime generation; (2) prefer well-tested library functions like `sqrt`; (3) profile-driven, never gut-feeling; (4) split complex ops into multiple small functions for readability + inlining eligibility; (5) memory access patterns often beat instruction-count optimization; (6) modern compilers (SSA, polyhedral) reduce manual-optimization payoff. **Closes Ch 12.**
